.

4.3.3
Analysis of Segmentation in CRATE

Segmentation emerges through minimalistic design.

Our empirical results demonstrate that self-supervised learning, as well as the specialized design options in DINO
(
Caron et al. 2021
)
(e.g., momentum encoder, student and teacher networks, self-distillation, etc.) are not necessary for the emergence of segmentation. We contend that an equally-promising approach to promote segmentation properties in transformer is to
design the transformer architecture with the structure of the input data in mind
. This finding of
crate
represents the
first supervised vision model with emergent segmentation properties
, and establishes white-box transformers as a promising direction for interpretable data-driven representation learning in foundation models.

Identifying semantic properties of attention heads.

We are interested in capturing the semantic meaning of certain attention
heads
; this is an important task for interpretability and is already studied for language transformers
(
Olsson et al. 2022
)
.
Intuitively, each head captures certain features of the data.
Given a
crate
model, we first forward an input image (e.g. a horse image as in
Figure

20
) and select four attention heads which seem to have semantic meaning by manual inspection. After identifying the attention heads, we visualize the self-attention map of these heads on other input images.
Interestingly, we find that each of the selected attention heads captures a different part of the object, and even a different semantic meaning.
For example, the attention head displayed in the first column of
Figure

20
captures the legs of different animals, and the attention head displayed in the last column captures the ears and head.
This parsing of the visual input into a part-whole hierarchy has been a fundamental goal of learning-based recognition architectures since deformable part models
(
Felzenszwalb and Huttenlocher 2005
;
Felzenszwalb et al. 2008
)
and capsule networks
(
Hinton et al. 2011
;
Sabour et al. 2017
)
—strikingly, it emerges from the white-box design of
crate
within our simple supervised training setup.

5
Conclusions and Open Directions

In this paper, we propose a new theoretical framework that allows us to derive a deep transformer-like network architecture, named
crate
, as an incremental optimization scheme to learn compressed and sparse representation of the input data, measured by a principled objective for information gain:
the sparse rate reduction
. The so-derived and learned deep architectures are not only fully mathematically interpretable but also consistent on a layer-by-layer level with their design objective.

Despite being arguably the simplest among all possible designs, these networks already demonstrate strong performance – slightly worse than existing black-box models – on large-scale real-world (image or text) datasets and tasks (discriminative and generative), and in supervised, unsupervised, and self-supervised settings.

In fact, recent follow-up work
(
Yang et al. 2024
)
suggested that one can significantly improve the performance of
crate
-like white-box models with a light training recipe on vision tasks, and to a large extent close the gap between white-box models such as
crate
and the conventional Transformers on some tasks.
Still, a gap persists. It would be interesting to investigate how to construct white-box models that can achieve same level of performance as black-box models, but we leave this for future work.

We believe this work truly helps
bridge the gap between theory and practice
of deep neural networks, as well as helps unify seemingly separate approaches to learning and representing data distributions through the perspective of data compression. These approaches include, but are not limited to: rate reduction, denoising-diffusion, and transformers. As we have seen, in these approaches, the role of each layer of the associated deep neural networks can be interpreted mathematically as operators to incrementally compress (denoise) and transform the data distribution according to its low-dimensional structures (modeled as Gaussian mixtures). In particular, our work provides a principled justification for slightly earlier empirical syntheses of some of these approaches, for example the recent success of Transformer-type architectures for denoising in the context of diffusion models
(
Peebles and Xie 2022
)
.

To a large extent, our work suggests that a universal way to effectively and efficiently learn a data distribution with intrinsically low-dimensional structures in a high-dimensional space is through
incremental compression
, as already stated as a slogan by
Wright and Ma 2022
:

We compress to learn, and we learn to compress.

This work and the previous work on ReduNet by
Chan et al. 2022
together strongly suggest that various deep neural networks are simply means to an end—compression. Different optimization schemes for compression are manifested as different network architectures and operators, e.g. LeNet, LISTA, ResNet, ReduNet, Transformers, which are now elucidated by CRATE.

From a practical standpoint, it is clear that this new framework can now provide us with theoretical guidelines to design and justify new, potentially more powerful, deep architectures for representation learning that are based on more advanced optimization techniques or strategies, instead of the basic gradient-based technique used to construct
crate
. Also, in this work, we have only used
crate
to learn deterministic autoencoding models. We believe that this framework can be extended to learn structured representations of the data distribution based on more advanced structured diffusion and denoising. This could potentially lead to more
controllable and consistent
generative methods, since improving the consistency and efficiency of existing diffusion-based generative models remains a challenging open problem
(
Song et al. 2023
)
.

Furthermore, as suggested by
Dai et al. 2022
;
Ma et al. 2022
, for any system to learn a low-dimensional data distribution
autonomously and continuously
, one needs to integrate the encoder and decoder not only as an autoencoder but as a
closed-loop transcription
:

\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69054ptf(\bm{x})\hskip 5.69054pt}\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69054ptg(\bm{z})\hskip 5.69054pt}\widehat{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69054ptf(\bm{x})\hskip 5.69054pt}\widehat{\bm{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(90)

which allows the system to correct itself by minimizing the discrepancy between the internal representations
\bm{z}
of the data and
\widehat{\bm{z}}
of the generated approximation, without any external help (by human) to compare between the data
\bm{x}
and
\widehat{\bm{x}}
. So far, the effectiveness of such a closed-loop transcription system has only been demonstrated with black-box deep architectures such as the ResNet
(
Dai et al. 2022
;
Tong et al. 2023
)
. It would be possible now to build a closed-loop transcription with purely white-box encoder and decoder designs. This could ultimately enable us to develop
complete
learning systems that are capable of learning autonomously and continuously an internal data representation that is fully mathematically interpretable, controllable, and eventually self-consistent, or “aligned”, with the true external data distribution. The so-learned representation, just like our acquired memory, can support both discriminative and generative tasks, and serve both recognition and prediction purposes.

Last but not least, we hope that this work provides an alternative viewpoint that may help clarify the ultimate capabilities of modern artificial intelligence (AI) systems, which are typically based on deep networks such as transformers. Just as with all other natural phenomena or technical innovations that were once “black boxes” to people, significant confusion and anxiety is arising in society about the potential or implications of emerging new AI systems, including the recent large language models (LLMs) such as GPT-4
(
OpenAI 2023b
)
, large image generation models such as Midjourney and DALL-E 3
(
Betker et al. 2023
)
, and many other multi-modal large language models (MLLMs)
(
Liu et al. 2023
;
OpenAI 2023a
)
.
From the perspective of this work, in which we developed a clear understanding of transformer-type learning systems, these large models are unlikely to do anything beyond purely mechanical data compression (encoding) and interpolation (decoding). That is, this work suggests that regarding essence of these existing large AI models, however magical and mysterious they might appear to be,

compression is all there is.

This leaves us with a much more profound and challenging question: whether
compression alone
is all one needs for a system, natural or artificial, to develop general intelligence or even sentience? We believe that compression, employed correctly, is merely the very first step of a long path towards general intelligence.

Appendix

Appendix A
Technical Details for
Section

2

A.1
Companion to
Section

2.3

We now give the derivation of the approximation alluded to in
Section

2.3
. In the process,
we make some simple approximations and technical assumptions. The validity of these assumptions may be explored, and the approximations refined, altogether providing a more complex (and possibly more performant) resulting self-attention like operator. For the sake of technical clarity and simplicity in this work, we make perhaps the
simplest possible choices
. As a result, we
do not
claim that our network is optimally designed, but rather that the principles we develop in this work (compression, denoising, sparsification, unrolled optimization) can provide the backbone for far superior and more interpretable network architectures in the future on sundry tasks. As it is, with our straightforward, simple, and interpretable design, we still obtain meaningful conceptual results and very solid empirical performance.

Recall that
\beta=p/(n\epsilon^{2})
.

Approximation 9
.

Let
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
have unit-norm columns, and
\bm{U}_{[K]}=(\bm{U}_{1},\dots,\bm{U}_{K})
such that each
\bm{U}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}
is an orthogonal matrix, the
(\bm{U}_{k})_{k=1}^{K}
are incoherent, and the columns of
\bm{Z}
approximately lie on
\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\mathrm{Span}(\bm{U}_{k})
. Let
\kappa>0
. Then

\bm{Z}-\kappa\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R^{c}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})\approx(1-\kappa\beta)\bm{Z}+\kappa\beta\operatorname{\texttt{MSSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]}),

(91)

where as in
Section

2.3
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\texttt{SSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{k})

\displaystyle=(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})\operatorname{softmax}\left((\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})^{\ast}(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})\right),

(92)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\texttt{MSSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})

\displaystyle=\beta\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1},\dots,\bm{U}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\operatorname{\texttt{SSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\operatorname{\texttt{SSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{K})\end{bmatrix},

(93)

where
\operatorname{softmax}\left(\cdot\right)
is the softmax operator (applied to each column of an input matrix), i.e.,

\displaystyle\operatorname{softmax}\left(\bm{v}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{v_{i}}}\begin{bmatrix}e^{v_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
e^{v_{n}}\end{bmatrix},

(94)

\displaystyle\operatorname{softmax}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{v}_{1},\dots,\bm{v}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\bm{v}_{1}\right),\dots,\operatorname{softmax}\left(\bm{v}_{K}\right)\end{bmatrix}.

(95)

{proof}

According to
Equation

31
, the gradient
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}{R}^{c}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})
is

\nabla_{\bm{Z}}{R}^{c}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})=\beta\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\left(\bm{I}+\beta(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})^{\ast}(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})\right)^{-1}.

(96)

Notice that according to
(
Chan et al. 2022
)
, the gradient is precisely the residual of a ridge regression for each (projected) token
\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{z}_{i}
using other projected tokens
\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{z}_{j}
as the regressors, hence being the residual of an auto-regression.

However, as we have seen in the work of ReduNet
(
Chan et al. 2022
)
, computing the inverse
\left(\bm{I}+\beta(\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z})^{*}(\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z})\right)^{-1}
can be expensive. Hence for computational efficiency, we may approximate it with the first order term of its von Neumann expansion:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}{R}^{c}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})

\displaystyle=\beta\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z}\Big({\bm{I}+\beta(\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z})^{*}(\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z})}\Big)^{-1}

(97)

\displaystyle\approx\beta\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z}\Big({\bm{I}-\beta(\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z})^{*}(\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z})}\Big)

(98)

\displaystyle=\beta\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\Big({\bm{U}^{*}_{k}\bm{Z}-\beta\bm{U}^{*}_{k}\bm{Z}[(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})^{\ast}(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})]}\Big)

(99)

Notice that the term
(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})^{\ast}(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})
is the auto-correlation among the projected tokens. As the tokens
\bm{Z}
may be from different subspaces, we would prefer to use only tokens that belong to the
same
subspace to regress and compress themselves. Hence we may convert the above correlation term into a subspace-membership indicator with a softmax operation, whence (
99
) becomes

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}{R}^{c}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\beta\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\Big({\bm{U}^{*}_{k}\bm{Z}-\beta\bm{U}^{*}_{k}\bm{Z}[(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})^{\ast}(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})]}\Big)

(100)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\beta\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z}-\beta^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\Big(\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z}\operatorname{softmax}\left((\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})^{\ast}(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})\right)\Big)

(101)

Then, we can rewrite the above approximation to the gradient of
R^{c}
as:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}{R}^{c}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})

\displaystyle\approx\beta\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z}-\beta^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\left(\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z}\operatorname{softmax}\left((\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})^{\ast}(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})\right)\right)

(102)

\displaystyle=\beta\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\bm{Z}-\beta^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\operatorname{\texttt{SSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{k})

(103)

\displaystyle=\beta\underbrace{\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{*}\right)\bm{Z}}_{\approx\bm{Z}}-\beta^{2}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1},\cdots,\bm{U}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\operatorname{\texttt{SSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\operatorname{\texttt{SSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{K})\end{bmatrix}

(104)

\displaystyle\approx\beta\bm{Z}-\beta^{2}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1},\cdots,\bm{U}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\operatorname{\texttt{SSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\operatorname{\texttt{SSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{K})\end{bmatrix}

(105)

\displaystyle=\beta\bm{Z}-\beta\operatorname{\texttt{MSSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})

(106)

Thus the gradient descent step with learning rate
\kappa>0
gives

\bm{Z}-\kappa\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R^{c}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})\approx(1-\kappa\beta)\bm{Z}+\kappa\beta\operatorname{\texttt{MSSA}}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]}).

(107)

A.2
Companion to
Section

2.4

In the main text
Section

2.4
, our connection between the second step of the alternating minimization and the LASSO optimization was high-level and heuristic. In some sense, the choice to pose the minimization step as a LASSO was a
simple, reliable, and interpretable choice
which works well in practice, but is nonetheless not backed up by rigorous theoretical justification.

In the following subsection, we provide a mathematical justification for a reformulation of the minimization step using a majorization-minimization framework. We further show that the associated unrolled optimization step bears a strong resemblance to the ISTA step.
This confirms our earlier discussion — we took the
simplest possible choice
in designing
crate
, but by more rigorous derivation we can uncover
alternative operators—which differ from the ISTA operator defined in (
43
) in Section
2.4
—
that nonetheless have the same conceptual function and may perform better in practice.

Assumptions.

In this section, we present a rigorous optimization
analysis of an incremental minimization approach to the objective

Equation

25
. We will show that under two simplifying assumptions,
namely

1.

The columns of
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
are normalized, in the sense that

\operatorname{diag}((\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2})^{\ast}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2})=\bm{1}
;
18
18

18

This is
a natural assumption in transformer-type architectures such as

crate
due to the use of LayerNorm blocks—although these blocks
(indeed, as we use them in
crate
) include trainable mean and
scale offsets as well as an additional mean subtraction operation

(
Phuong and Hutter 2022
)
, they are initialized to have zero mean and
unit norm, hence this assumption corresponds to an analysis of the
network at its initialization.

2.

We have
d\geq n
,
19
19

19

This assumption is without loss
of generality, as we will see in the analysis below. The reason is that

\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}
and
\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\ast}
have the same nonzero eigenvalues
regardless of the shape of
\bm{Z}
, which implies that
\log\det(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z})=\log\det(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\ast})
. In particular,
interpreting the norms appropriately (with a slight abuse of notation), we
have
\varphi(\bm{Z})=\varphi(\bm{Z}^{\ast})
, so for the purposes of analysis
we can always proceed as though
\bm{Z}
is a tall matrix (as long as
we do not use any special properties of
\alpha
in our
derivation).
and the columns of
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
are
orthogonal, so that
(\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2})^{\ast}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}=\bm{I}
.
20
20

20

This assumption is strictly stronger than the previous
one, and strictly stronger than an assumption of incoherence on the
columns. It corresponds to the representation
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
being non-collapsed, which we expect to hold at initialization due to the projections
\bm{U}_{[K]}
being random.

the approach leads to an update iteration that is equal to a slightly
simplified version of the ISTA block
Equation

43
. We see this as a
justification for our derivation in
Section

2.4
, which obtained the ISTA
block by introducing an additional simplifying assumption on the distribution
of the data at layer
\ell
.

Analysis.

Following
Equation

42
, we will consider
the natural relaxation of the
\ell^{0}
“norm” to the
\ell^{1}
norm, and
incorporate a nonnegativity constraint. Consider the objective

\varphi(\bm{Z})=\lambda\lVert\bm{Z}\rVert_{1}+\chi_{\{\bm{Z}\geq\mathbf{0}\}}(\bm{Z})-\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\log\det\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)}_{R(\bm{Z})},

(108)

where
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
and
\alpha=d/(n\varepsilon^{2})
, and

\chi_{\{\bm{Z}\geq\mathbf{0}\}}
denotes the characteristic function for the set
of elementwise-nonnegative matrices
\bm{Z}
. As in

Section

A.1
, we calculate

\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z})=\alpha\bm{Z}\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}.

(109)

We consider an incremental optimization scheme for the highly nonlinear and
nonconvex objective
\varphi
. Following
Section

2.3
, we optimize
locally at a “post-compression” iterate
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
. We follow the
standard proximal majorize-minimize framework
(
Wright and Ma 2022
)
for
incremental/local optimization: this begins with the second-order Taylor
expansion for the smooth part of
\varphi
in a neighborhood of the
current iterate
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
:

\begin{split}R(\bm{Z})=R(\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2})&+\left\langle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}),\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}\right\rangle\\
&+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\left\langle\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2},\nabla^{2}R(\bm{Z}_{t})\left(\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}\right)\right\rangle\ \mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

(110)

where for any
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
,
\bm{Z}_{t}=t\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}+(1-t)\bm{Z}
. The proximal majorization-minimization approach alternates two steps to
minimize
\varphi
:

1.

First, use assumptions on
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
to derive an upper bound
on the operator norm of the Hessian
\nabla^{2}R(\bm{Z})
over the
effective domain of the optimization problem. We will write
L
for
this (uniform) upper bound. This yields a quadratic upper bound for the
smooth part of the objective
\varphi
.

2.

Then, alternately minimize the
smooth part
of the quadratic
upper bound as a function of
\bm{Z}
, and take a
proximal step

on the nonsmooth part. It can be shown
(
Wright and Ma 2022
)
that
corresponds to the iteration

\bm{Z}^{+}={\operatorname{prox}}_{\frac{\lambda}{L}(\lVert\>\cdot\>\rVert_{1}+\chi_{\{\bm{Z}\geq\mathbf{0}\}})}\left(\bm{Z}+\frac{1}{L}\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z})\right)

(111)

In the alternating minimization setting of this paper for optimizing

Equation

15
, we only take one such step, starting at

\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
.

We will instantiate this program below, showing quantitative error bounds
related to our assumptions above as necessary. Rather than directly applying
the iteration
Equation

111
, we will derive it below under our
aforementioned assumptions.

Starting at
Equation

110
, our first task is to upper bound the
quadratic residual.
This corresponds to estimating

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2},\nabla^{2}R(\bm{Z}_{t})\left(\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}\right)\right\rangle

(112)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}R(\bm{Z}_{t})\right\rVert_{\ell^{2}\to\ell^{2}}\left\lVert\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

(113)

with Cauchy-Schwarz.
Using

Lemma

10
, we can estimate the operator norm term in the
previous bound in terms of properties of
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
. We need to bound

\alpha\sup_{\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{F}\leq 1}\left\lVert\left(\bm{\Delta}-\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}(\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})\right)(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F},

(114)

and
Lemma

11
gives that this term is no larger than

9\alpha/4
for any
\bm{Z}
and any
t
.
With this
estimate and
Equation

110
, we have a quadratic upper bound for

-R(\bm{Z})
:

-R(\bm{Z})\leq-R(\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2})+\left\langle-\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}),\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}\right\rangle\\
+\frac{9\alpha}{8}\left\lVert\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}.

(115)

Meanwhile, by our assumptions above, we have

-\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2})=-\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{I}\right)^{-1}=-\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}.

(116)

We now minimize the preceding quadratic upper bound as a
function of
\bm{Z}
. Differentiating, the minimizer
\bm{Z}_{\mathrm{opt}}
is
calculated as

\bm{Z}_{\mathrm{opt}}=\left(1+\frac{4}{9(1+\alpha)}\right)\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2},

(117)

and it is well-known that the proximal operator of the sum of

\chi_{\{\bm{Z}\geq\mathbf{0}\}}
and
\lambda\lVert\>\cdot\>\rVert_{1}
is simply the one-sided
soft-thresholding operator
(
Wright and Ma 2022
)

{\operatorname{prox}}_{\chi_{\{\bm{Z}\geq\mathbf{0}\}}+\lambda\lVert\>\cdot\>\rVert_{1}}\left(\bm{Z}\right)=\max\{\bm{Z}-\lambda\mathbf{1},\mathbf{0}\},

(118)

where the maximum is applied elementwise. As in
Section

2.4
, we may write
this elementwise maximum simply as
\operatorname{ReLU}
. Thus, one step of
proximal majorization-minimization under our simplifying assumptions takes the form

\bm{Z}^{\ell+1}=\operatorname{ReLU}\left(\left(1+\frac{4}{9(1+\alpha)}\right)\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}-\frac{4\lambda}{9\alpha}\mathbf{1}\right),

(119)

Finally, we point out one additional elaboration which introduces the
dictionary
\bm{D}
that appears in the ISTA block in
Section

2.4
.
Notice that for any orthogonal
\bm{D}
, one has
R(\bm{D}\bm{Z})=R(\bm{Z})
for every

\bm{Z}
. This symmetry implies equivariance properties of
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z})

and
\nabla^{2}_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z})
: for every
\bm{Z}
and every
\bm{\Delta}
and every
orthogonal
\bm{D}
,

\displaystyle\bm{D}\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{D}\bm{Z}),

(120)

\displaystyle\langle\bm{D}\bm{\Delta},\nabla^{2}_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z})\left(\bm{D}\bm{\Delta}\right)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\bm{\Delta},\nabla^{2}_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{D}\bm{Z})\left(\bm{\Delta}\right)\rangle.

(121)

Hence the quadratic Taylor expansion
Equation

110
can be
written equivalently as

\begin{split}R(\bm{Z})=R(\bm{D}^{\ast}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2})&+\left\langle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{D}^{\ast}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}),\bm{Z}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bm{D}^{\ast}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}}\right\rangle\\
&+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\left\langle\bm{Z}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bm{D}^{\ast}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}},\nabla^{2}R(\bm{D}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})\left(\bm{Z}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bm{D}^{\ast}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}}\right)\right\rangle\ \mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

(122)

for any orthogonal
\bm{D}
. The significance of this is that we have obtained an
expression equivalent to
Equation

110
, but with

\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
replaced by
\bm{D}^{\ast}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
; moreover, because our
approximation arguments above are not affected by left-multiplication of

\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
by an orthogonal matrix (this operation does not change the
norms of the columns of
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
, or their correlations, and hence the
matrix’s incoherence), we can apply exactly the same line of reasoning above to
obtain that an equivalent proximal majorization-minimization iteration is given
by

\bm{Z}^{\ell+1}=\operatorname{ReLU}\left(\left(1+\frac{4}{9(1+\alpha)}\right)\bm{D}^{\ast}\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}-\frac{4\lambda}{9\alpha}\mathbf{1}\right),

(123)

for any orthogonal dictionary
\bm{D}
. This gives an update quite similar to the
ISTA block
Equation

43
in the case where the dictionary used in

Section

2.4
is orthogonal, but without a skip connection.

The operator defined in (
123
) is not exactly the same as the ISTA operator defined in (
43
) but exhibits substantial similarities.

We thus obtain a natural
white-box version of this part of the architecture, along with the natural
interpretation
that its purpose is to sparsify the compressed tokens

\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}

with respect to
a (learnable) dictionary
, which accords with recent
empirical studies
(
Li et al. 2023b
)
.

Other architectures?

As we mentioned at the start of this section,
the preceding derivation is performed in the most elementary possible setting
in order to demonstrate the majorization-minimization approach for layer
design. More precise approximations or assumptions may lead to superior layer
designs that better optimize the target objective
Equation

15
(and in
particular
Equation

25
). We mention two here:

1.

Beyond exactly-incoherent features
: our derivations above assumed that the
incoming representations
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
were already maximal for the
expansion term
R
in
Equation

25
. It is desirable to obtain
a ‘perturbative’ derivation, which applies in cases where

\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2}
is not fully orthogonal, but instead near-orthogonal,
in particular
incoherent

(
Wright and Ma 2022
)
. The
derivations above can be adapted to this setting; the perturbation
bounds become slightly more delicate, and the ultimate layer

Equation

123
changes to involve additional
normalization.

2.

Beyond orthogonal dictionaries
: The symmetries of the
expansion term
R
in
Equation

25
may be followed to lead to
a pair of dictionaries
\bm{D}
and
\bm{D}^{\prime}
and an objective that
sparsifies
\bm{D}\bm{Z}\bm{D}^{\prime}
. This type of transformation is suggestive of
popular architectures that mix over tokens

(
Tolstikhin et al. 2021
;
Trockman et al. 2023
)
, however we consider the
simpler form
\bm{D}\bm{Z}
in this work. In addition, we have
focused for simplicity on orthogonal dictionaries
\bm{D}
; as in the
previous bullet, one may consider in a similar way dictionaries
\bm{D}

which are complete and near-orthogonal. Adapting the derivation to

overcomplete dictionaries
is an interesting future direction
that we expect to improve the scalability of
crate
; one avenue to
achieve this could be increasing the number of projections
\bm{U}_{[K]}

and their embedding dimensions.

A.2.1
Auxiliary Lemmas

Lemma 10
.

Consider the function

R(\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{2}\log\det\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right),

(124)

where
\alpha>0
is a constant. Then we have

\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z})=\alpha\bm{Z}\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1},

(125)

and the Hessian operator
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{2}R(\bm{Z})\colon\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
satisfies that for any
\bm{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
,

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z})\left(\bm{\Delta}\right)

(126)

\displaystyle=\alpha\bm{\Delta}\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}-\alpha^{2}\bm{Z}\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}\left(\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}.

(127)

{proof}

The gradient calculation follows from
(
Yu et al. 2020
)
, for example.
For the Hessian, we use the usual approach to calculating derivatives:
if
\bm{\Delta}
is any matrix with the same shape as
\bm{Z}
and
t>0
,

\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{2}R(\bm{Z})\left(\bm{\Delta}\right)={\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}\left[t\mapsto\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta})\right],

(128)

valid since
R
is smooth. We have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta})

(129)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta})\left(\bm{I}+\alpha(\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta})^{\ast}(\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta})\right)^{-1}

(130)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta})\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}+\alpha t\left[\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\right]\right)^{-1}

(131)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta})\left(\bm{I}+\alpha t\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}\left[\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\right]\right)^{-1}\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}

(132)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta})\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-\alpha t)^{k}\left(\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}\left[\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\right]\right)^{k}\right)\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1},

(133)

where in the fourth line we require that
t
is sufficiently close to

0
in order to invoke the Neumann series. First, notice that the term
involving
\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}
does not play a role in the final
expression: after we differentiate with respect to
t
and take a limit

t\to 0
, terms arising due to differentiation of
t\mapsto t\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}
go to zero, because whenever the summation index

k>0
we have a term
(-\alpha t)^{k}
that goes to zero as
t\to 0
.
We thus obtain with the product rule

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}\left[t\mapsto\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R(\bm{Z}+t\bm{\Delta})\right]

(134)

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle\alpha\bm{\Delta}\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}-\alpha^{2}\bm{Z}\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}\left(\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)\left(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}.

(135)

Lemma 11
.

One has

\sup_{\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{F}\leq 1}\left\lVert\left(\bm{\Delta}-\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}(\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})\right)(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\frac{9}{4}.

(136)

{proof}

Fix
\bm{\Delta}
satisfying
\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{F}\leq 1
. By the
triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(\bm{\Delta}-\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}(\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})\right)(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}

(137)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}+\alpha\left\lVert\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}(\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}.

(138)

For the first term, we note that

\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}=\left\lVert\left((\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\otimes\bm{I}\right)\operatorname{vec}(\bm{\Delta})\right\rVert_{F},

(139)

and since
(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\preceq\bm{I}
,
we obtain from Cauchy-Schwarz
21
21

21

Recall that the eigenvalues of a Kronecker product
of symmetric matrices are the tensor product of the eigenvalues
(with multiplicity).

\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{F}.

(140)

We can use a similar idea to control the second term. We have from the
triangle inequality

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}(\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}

(141)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\lVert\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}

(142)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\right\rVert_{F}.

(143)

For the first term, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}

(144)

\displaystyle\quad=\left\lVert\left((\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\otimes\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\right)\operatorname{vec}(\bm{\Delta})\right\rVert_{F}

(145)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sigma_{\max}\left((\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right)\sigma_{\max}\left(\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\right)\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{F}

(146)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{F}.

(147)

The last estimate follows from a computation using the SVD of
\bm{Z}_{t}
.
Meanwhile, we have for the second term by a similar argument (using the
fact that the singular values of
\bm{A}
and
\bm{A}^{\ast}
are identical for
any matrix
\bm{A}
)

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\right\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{\max}\left((\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\right)^{2}\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{F}

(148)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\alpha}\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{F},

(149)

where once again the estimate follows from a computation involving the
SVD of
\bm{Z}_{t}
(together with the fact that the function
\sigma\mapsto\sigma/(1+\alpha\sigma^{2})
is bounded on
\sigma\geq 0

by
1/(2\sqrt{\alpha})
). Putting it together, we have obtained

\left\lVert\left(\bm{\Delta}-\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}(\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}+\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})\right)(\bm{I}+\alpha\bm{Z}_{t}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{t})^{-1}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\frac{9}{4}\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert_{F},

(150)

which gives the claim after taking suprema.

Appendix B
Technical Details for
Section

3

B.1
An Overview of Diffusion Processes

In this section, we give an overview of the basics of time-reversible Itô diffusion processes, the mathematical foundation for diffusion models. This is to make this paper more self-contained by providing knowledge about general diffusion processes that we will apply to our special models. The coverage adapts that of
Millet et al. 1989b
;
Song et al. 2021b
;
Karras et al. 2022
.

Consider a generic Itô diffusion process
(\bm{z}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
, where
\bm{z}(t)
is an
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued random variable, given by the SDE

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{z}(t)=b(\bm{z}(t),t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\Sigma(\bm{z}(t),t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(t),\qquad\bm{z}(0)\sim P,\qquad\forall t\in[0,T]

(151)

where
\bm{w}
is a Brownian motion and
P
is some probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
(in this case representing the data distribution). Here the
drift coefficient

b\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
diffusion coefficient

\Sigma\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
are functions. To make sense of
Equation

151
and also verify the existence of strong (i.e., pathwise well-defined) solutions, we need some regularity on them, and we choose the following assumption:

A1.

b
and
\Sigma
have some spatial smoothness and do not grow too fast, i.e., there is a constant
K\geq 0
such that for all
\bm{x},\widetilde{\bm{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left[\lVert\Sigma(\bm{x},t)-\Sigma(\widetilde{\bm{z}},t)\rVert_{F}+\lVert b(\bm{x},t)-b(\widetilde{\bm{z}},t)\rVert_{2}\right]\leq K\lVert\bm{x}-\widetilde{\bm{z}}\rVert_{2}

(152)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left[\lVert\Sigma(\bm{x},t)\rVert_{F}+\lVert b(\bm{x},t)\rVert_{2}\right]\leq K(1+\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{2}).

(153)

In general,
\bm{z}(t)
may not have a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. For example, suppose that
P
is supported on some low-dimensional linear subspace (or even a Dirac delta measure), and take
\Sigma
to be the orthoprojector onto this subspace. Then
\bm{z}(t)
will be supported on this subspace for all
t
and thus not have a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure. Thus, when further discussing processes of the type
Equation

151
, we make the following assumption

A2.

\bm{z}(t)
has a probability density function
p(\cdot,t)
for all
t>0
.

This is guaranteed by either of the following conditions
(
Millet et al. 1989b
)
:

A2.1

b
and
\Sigma
are differentiable in
(\bm{x},t)
and have Hölder-continuous derivatives, and
P
has a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure;

A2.2

The event

\{\text{$\operatorname{rank}(\Sigma(\bm{z}(s),s))=m$ for all $s$ in some neighborhood of $0$}\}

(154)

happens
P
-almost surely.

Define
\Psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
by

\Psi(\bm{x},t)\doteq\Sigma(\bm{x},t)\Sigma(\bm{x},t)^{\ast}.

(155)

To discuss time-reversibility, we also need the following local integrability condition, which is another measure of sharp growth of the coefficients (or precisely their derivatives):

A3.

The functions
(\bm{x},t)\mapsto\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\Psi(\bm{x},t)p(\bm{x},t))
are integrable on sets of the form
D\times[t_{0},1]
for
t_{0}>0
and
D
a bounded measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
:

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{1}\int_{D}\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\Psi(\bm{x},t)p(\bm{x},t))\rVert_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{x}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

(156)

To write the notation out more explicitly,

(157)

where
\Psi^{i}
isthe
i^{\mathrm{th}}
rowof
\Psi
transposedtoacolumn
,
and
\Psi^{ij}
isthe
(i,j)^{\mathrm{th}}
entryof
\Psi
.
Notethat
Millet et al. 1989b
phrasesthisintermsofanlocalintegrabilityconditiononeach
\lvert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\Psi^{i}(\bm{x},t)p(\bm{x},t))\rvert
,
whichwouldnaturallygivealocalintegrabilityconditionon
\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\Psi(\bm{x},t)p(\bm{x},t))\rVert_{\infty}
.
However
,
allnormson
\mathbb{R}^{m}
areequivalent
,
andsothisleadstoalocalintegrabilityconditionfor
\lVert\nabla_{x}\cdot(\Psi(\bm{x},t)p(\bm{x},t))\rVert_{2}
asproduced
.
Notethattheassumptionsdonotguaranteethattheinvolvedderivativesexist
,
inwhichcasetheyaretakeninthedistributional
(

)

,

(

)
.

Noticethatthedeterminsticflowsareactuallygradientflowsonthescore
,
whichconcretelyrevealsaconnectionbetweensamplingandoptimization
,
andthusbetweendiffusionmodels
(
preciselythosewhichusetheprobabilityflowODEtosample
)
andunrolledoptimizationnetworks
.

Inthiscontext
,
wecanalsoestablishtheconnectionbetweendiffusionnetworksanditerativedenoising
.
Inthevariance
-
explodingsetting
,
wehave

\bm{z}(t)\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{z}(0),2t\bm{I}),

(161)

whichcanbeeasilycomputedusingresultsfrom
,
e
.
g
.,
Särkkä and Solin 2019
.
Thus
\bm{z}(t)
isanoisyversionof
\bm{z}(0)
,
withnoiselevelincreasingmonotonicallywith
t
,
andsampling
\bm{z}(0)
from
\bm{z}(t)
conceptuallyremovesthisnoise
.
Concretely
,
Tweedie’s formula
(
Efron 2011b
)
saysthattheoptimaldenoisingfunction
\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}(0)\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\bm{z}(t)]
hasasimpleformintermsofthescorefunction
:

\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}(0)\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\bm{z}(t)]=\bm{z}(t)+2t\cdot s(\bm{z}(t),t).

(162)

Inotherwords
,
thescorefunction
s
pointsfromthecurrentiterate
\bm{z}(t)
tothevalueoftheoptimaldenoisingfunction
,
soitisanegativemultipleoftheconditionally
-
expectednoise
.
Followingthescoreby
(
stochastic
)
gradientfloworitsdiscretizationisthusequivalenttoiterativedenoising
.

B.2
Companion to
Section

3.1

Here we produce the approximation result alluded to in
Section

3.1
.
To simplify the proofs, we use the following notation correspondences:
\bm{x}\mapsto\bm{z}^{\ell}
,
\bm{z}\mapsto\bm{z}_{\natural}^{\ell}
, and
\sigma\mapsto\sigma^{\ell}
.

Approximation 12
.

Suppose that
\bm{z}
follows the low-dimensional Gaussian mixture model
Equation

10
with respect to subspaces
\bm{U}_{[K]}
, so that
\bm{x}=\bm{z}+\sigma\bm{w}
, where
\bm{w}
is a standard Gaussian vector independent of
\bm{z}
, follows
Equation

11
. That is, for some random index
s\sim\pi
where
\pi
is a distribution on
[K]
and
s
is chosen independently of all other randomness, we have

\bm{z}=\bm{U}_{s}\bm{\alpha},

(163)

where
\bm{\alpha}
is a zero-mean Gaussian vector with diagonal covariance
\bm{\Lambda}
. For each
k\in[K]
, define

\bm{\Sigma}_{i}\doteq\operatorname{Cov}[\bm{z}\mid s=k]=\bm{U}_{k}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}.

(164)

Assume (for the sake of normalization) that

\frac{\pi_{i}}{\sqrt{\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{i}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I})}}=\frac{\pi_{j}}{\sqrt{\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{j}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I})}},\qquad\text{for all}\ 1\leq i\leq j\leq K.

(165)

Then we have

\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}\mid\bm{x}]\approx\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1},\dots,\bm{U}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\left[\operatorname{diag}\left(\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{U}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{U}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right)\otimes\bm{I}_{p}\right]\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{U}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\end{bmatrix},

(166)

where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product, i.e., the block matrix defined by

\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{11}\bm{B}&\cdots&A_{1n}\bm{B}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
A_{m1}\bm{B}&\cdots&A_{mn}\bm{B}\end{bmatrix}.

(167)

{proof}

Define
\bm{M}_{k}\doteq(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I})^{-1/2}
. From
Proposition

14
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log q(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{M}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{M}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}

(168)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\sigma\bm{M}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\sigma\bm{M}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}

(169)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lVert\sigma\bm{M}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lVert\sigma\bm{M}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}.

(170)

Now define
\bm{P}_{k}\doteq\bm{I}_{d}-\sigma\bm{M}_{k}
, and let
\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-p)}
be an orthogonal complement of
\bm{U}_{k}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{k}

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{d}-\sigma\bm{M}_{k}

(171)

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{d}-\sigma\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d}\right)^{-1/2}

(172)

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{d}-\sigma\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Lambda}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\\
(\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp})^{\ast}\end{bmatrix}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d}\right)^{-1/2}

(173)

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{d}-\sigma\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Lambda}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{p}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d-p}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\\
(\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp})^{\ast}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{-1/2}

(174)

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{d}-\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sigma(\bm{\Lambda}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{p})^{-1/2}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\sigma\cdot(\sigma^{2})^{-1/2}\bm{I}_{d-p}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\\
(\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp})^{\ast}\end{bmatrix}

(175)

\displaystyle=\bm{I}_{d}-\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(\sigma^{-2}\bm{\Lambda}+\bm{I}_{p})^{-1/2}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{I}_{d-p}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\\
(\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp})^{\ast}\end{bmatrix}

(176)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{p}-(\sigma^{-2}\bm{\Lambda}+\bm{I}_{p})^{-1/2}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\\
(\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp})^{\ast}\end{bmatrix}

(177)

\displaystyle\approx\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}&\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{p}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\\
(\bm{U}_{k}^{\perp})^{\ast}\end{bmatrix}

(178)

\displaystyle=\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}.

(179)

Thus
\bm{P}_{k}
is approximately a projection when
\sigma
is small. Under this algebraic relation, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log q(\bm{x})

(180)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lVert\sigma\bm{M}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lVert\sigma\bm{M}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}

(181)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lVert(\bm{I}_{d}-\bm{P}_{1})^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lVert(\bm{I}_{d}-\bm{P}_{K})^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)(\bm{I}_{d}-\bm{P}_{k})(\bm{I}_{d}-\bm{P}_{k})^{\ast}\bm{x}

(182)

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{P}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{P}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)(\bm{I}_{d}-\bm{P}_{k})(\bm{I}_{d}-\bm{P}_{k})^{\ast}\bm{x}

(183)

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{P}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{P}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)(\bm{I}_{d}-\bm{P}_{k})^{*}\bm{x}

(184)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\bm{x}}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{P}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{P}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{P}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{P}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\bm{P}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}

(185)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{x}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{P}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{P}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\bm{P}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}

(186)

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{x}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{U}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{U}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}

(187)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bm{x}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1},\cdots,\bm{U}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\left[\operatorname{diag}\left(\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{U}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{U}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right)\otimes\bm{I}_{p}\right]\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{U}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\end{bmatrix}.

(188)

Plugging this into Tweedie’s formula, we have

\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}\mid\bm{x}]\approx\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1},\cdots,\bm{U}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\left[\operatorname{diag}\left(\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{U}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{U}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right)\otimes\bm{I}_{p}\right]\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{U}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\end{bmatrix}.

(189)

Remark 13
.

Although
12
is stated as an approximation rather than as a proposition, we believe it should be possible without too much extra work to convert it into a statement of asymptotic equivalence as
\sigma\to 0
(in particular, holding for
\sigma
below the smallest (nonzero) eigenvalue of
\bm{\Lambda}
). Most approximations taken in the derivation of
12
can immediately be turned into asymptotic claims; the only slightly delicate point is treating the softmax, which can be accomplished using standard “high temperature” convergence behavior of the softmax function

(
Gao and Pavel 2017
)

(in particular, as
\sigma\to 0
in our expressions, the softmax concentrates on the “best head”).

B.2.1
Auxiliary Lemmas

Proposition 14
.

Suppose that
\bm{z}
follows the low-dimensional Gaussian mixture model
Equation

10
with respect to subspaces
\bm{U}_{[K]}
, so that
\bm{x}=\bm{z}+\sigma\bm{w}
, where
\bm{w}
is a standard Gaussian vector independent of
\bm{z}
, follows
Equation

11
. That is, for some random index
s\sim\pi
where
\pi
is a distribution on
[K]
and
s
is chosen independently of all other randomness, we have

\bm{z}=\bm{U}_{s}\bm{\alpha},

(190)

where
\bm{\alpha}
is a zero-mean Gaussian vector with diagonal covariance
\bm{\Lambda}
. For each
k\in[K]
, define

\bm{\Sigma}_{i}\doteq\operatorname{Cov}[\bm{z}\mid s=k]=\bm{U}_{k}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast},\qquad\bm{M}_{k}\doteq(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I})^{-1/2}.

(191)

Assume (for the sake of normalization) that

\pi_{i}\det(\bm{M}_{i})=\pi_{j}\det(\bm{M}_{j}),\qquad\text{for all}\ 1\leq i\leq j\leq K.

(192)

Then, letting
\bm{x}\mapsto q(\bm{x})
be the density of
\bm{x}
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log q(\bm{x})

(193)

\displaystyle=-\begin{bmatrix}\bm{M}_{1},\cdots,\bm{M}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\left[\operatorname{diag}\left(\operatorname{softmax}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{M}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{M}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right)\otimes\bm{I}_{d}\right]\begin{bmatrix}\bm{M}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{M}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\end{bmatrix}.

(194)

{proof}

By the law of total probability, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log q(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log\sum_{k=1}^{K}q(\bm{x}\mid k)\pi_{k}

(195)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\nabla_{\bm{x}}q(\bm{x}\mid k)}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}q(\bm{x}\mid k)\pi_{k}}

(196)

where
q(\bm{x}\mid k)
is the conditional density of
\bm{x}
given the event
\{s=k\}
. To compute this quantity, note that
conditional on the value of
s
, we have

\bm{x}=\bm{z}_{s}+\sigma\bm{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{s}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d}).

(197)

Thus we have

q(\bm{x}\mid k)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d})}}\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\ast}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d})^{-1}\bm{x}\right),

(198)

This gives

\nabla_{\bm{x}}q(\bm{x}\mid k)=-q(\bm{x}\mid k)\cdot(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d})^{-1}\bm{x}.

(199)

Putting this all together, we get

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log q(\bm{x})

(200)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}q(\bm{x}\mid k)\pi_{k}\cdot(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d})^{-1}\bm{x}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}q(\bm{x}\mid k)\pi_{k}}

(201)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d})^{-1/2}\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\ast}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d})^{-1}\bm{x}\right)\cdot(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d})^{-1}\bm{x}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\det(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d})^{-1/2}\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\ast}(\bm{\Sigma}_{k}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d})^{-1}\bm{x}\right)}

(202)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\det(\bm{M}_{k})\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\ast}\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}\right)\cdot\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\det(\bm{M}_{k})\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\ast}\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}\right)}

(203)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\det(\bm{M}_{k})\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\cdot\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\det(\bm{M}_{k})\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\ast}\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}\right)}.

(204)

Given our assumption that
each
\pi_{k}\det(\bm{M}_{k})
is the same, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log q(\bm{x})

(205)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\det(\bm{M}_{k})\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\cdot\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\det(\bm{M}_{k})\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)}

(206)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\cdot\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)}

(207)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{e}_{k}^{\ast}\operatorname{softmax}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{M}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{M}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\bm{M}_{k}\bm{M}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{x}

(208)

\displaystyle=-\begin{bmatrix}\bm{M}_{1},\dots,\bm{M}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\left[\operatorname{diag}\left(\operatorname{softmax}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}\lVert\bm{M}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\lVert\bm{M}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right)\otimes\bm{I}_{d}\right]\begin{bmatrix}\bm{M}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{x}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{M}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{x}\end{bmatrix}.

(209)

B.3
Companion to
Section

3.2

In this section, we prove a formal version of the result
Theorem

6
stated in
Section

3.2
. That is, we examine a basic yet representative instantiation of the signal model
Equation

11
, and show that under this model, in a
natural regime of parameter scales motivated by the architecture of
crate

applied to standard image classification benchmarks, the operation implemented
by taking a gradient step on the compression term of the sparse rate reduction
objective
Equation

15
corresponds to a projection operation at
quantization scales
\varepsilon^{2}
proportional to the size of the deviation.
This leads us in particular to a formal version of the result

Theorem

6
.

Signal model.

We consider an instantiation of the model
Equation

11
, elaborated here. That is, we fix a distribution over tokens
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
induced by
the following natural signal model: each token
\bm{z}_{i}
is drawn independently from the
normalized isotropic Gaussian measure on one of
K

p
-dimensional
subspaces with orthonormal bases
\bm{U}_{1},\dots,\bm{U}_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}
,
22
22

22

More precisely,
\bm{z}_{i}
is distributed according to the
pushforward of the normalized isotropic Gaussian measure
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\tfrac{1}{p}\bm{I})
on
\mathbb{R}^{p}
by the bases
\bm{U}_{k}
.

which comprise the low-dimensional structure in the observed tokens, then
corrupted with i.i.d. Gaussian noise
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{d}\bm{I})
;
the subspace each token is drawn from is selected uniformly at random,
independently of all other randomness in the problem.
This signal model therefore corresponds to the setting of uncorrelated tokens,
with maximum entropy coordinate distributions within subspaces.
It is natural to first develop our
theoretical understanding of the connection between compression and the score
function in the uncorrelated setting, although in general, the ability of
crate
to capture correlations in the
data through the MSSA block is essential. In connection with the latter issue, we note that our proofs will generalize straightforwardly to the setting of “well-dispersed” correlated tokens: see the discussion in
Remark

18
.

We make the further following assumptions within this model:

1.

Inspired by an ablation in
Figure

17
, which suggests that the learned

crate
model on supervised classification on Imagenet has signal models
\bm{U}_{k}

which are near-incoherent, we will assume that the subspaces
\bm{U}_{k}
have pairwise orthogonal column spaces.
Our proofs will generalize straightforwardly to the setting where the subspaces are merely incoherent: see the discussion in
Remark

18
.

2.

We assume that the relative scales of these parameters conform to the

crate
-Base settings, trained on Imagenet: from

Table

9
, these parameters are

(a)

d=768
;

(b)

n=196
;

(c)

K=12
;

(d)

p=d/K=64
.

In particular,
d\gg n\gg p
and
Kp=d
.

These precise parameter values will not play a role in our analysis. We merely require the following quantitative relationships between the parameter values, which are more general than the above precise settings.

Assumption 15
.

We have
\varepsilon\leq 1
,
\bm{U}_{k}^{\top}\bm{U}_{k^{\prime}}={\mathds{1}}_{k=k^{\prime}}\bm{I}
for all
k\neq k^{\prime}
, and the following parameter settings and scales:

•

d\geq n\geq p\geq K\geq 2
;

•

Kp=d
;

•

C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\leq\frac{1}{2}n/K
, where
C_{1}
is the same as the universal constant
C_{1}
in the statement of
Proposition

25
;

•

6C_{2}^{2}n\leq d
, where
C_{2}
is the same as the universal constant
C_{3}
in the statement of
Proposition

28
;

•

2C_{4}^{2}n\leq d
, where
C_{4}
is the same as the universal constant
C_{1}
in
Proposition

24
;

note:
there is no self-reference, as the third inequality is not used to prove
Proposition

25
, the fourth is not used to prove
Proposition

28
, and the fifth is not used to prove
Proposition

24
.

The first and second inequalities together imply in particular that
p\geq n/K
. The third inequality implies that
C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}<n/K
. The first, second, and and third inequalities together imply that
p>C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}
, and that
0<n/K-C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}<n/K<n/K+C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}<n
.

These inequalities are verifiable in practice if one wishes to explicitly compute the absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}
, and indeed they hold for our
crate
-Base model.

Formally, let
\mu(K,p,\sigma^{2})
denote the probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}

corresponding to the noisy Gaussian mixture distribution specified above.
We let
\bm{Z}_{o}\sim\mu
denote an observation distributed according to
this signal model: formally, there exists a (random) map
i\mapsto s_{i}
, for
i\in[n]
and
s_{i}\in[K]
, such that

\bm{z}_{oi}=\bm{U}_{s_{i}}\bm{\alpha}_{i}+\bm{\delta}_{i},\quad i=1,\dots,n,

(210)

where
\bm{\Delta}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\delta}_{n}\end{bmatrix}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{d}\bm{I})
, and (independently)
\bm{\alpha}_{i}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\tfrac{1}{p}\bm{I})
.
It is convenient to write this observation model in block form.
To this end, let
K_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathds{1}}_{s_{i}=k}
for
k\in[K]

denote the number of times the
k
-th subspace is represented amongst the
columns of
\bm{Z}_{o}
(a random variable).
Then by rotational invariance of the Gaussian distribution, we have

\bm{Z}_{o}\overset{d}{=}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{1}\bm{A}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{U}_{K}\bm{A}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\bm{\Pi}+\bm{\Delta},

(211)

where
\overset{d}{=}
denotes equality in distribution,
\bm{\Pi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is
a uniformly random permutation matrix, and each
\bm{A}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times K_{k}}
. We also define
\bm{X}_{\natural}
to be the noise-free version of
\bm{Z}_{o}
.

Because of this equality in distribution, we will commit the mild abuse of
notation of identifying the block representation

Equation

211
with the observation model

Equation

210
that follows the distribution
\mu
.

Denoising in the uncorrelated tokens model.

In the uncorrelated tokens model
Equation

211
, the marginal
distribution of each column of
\bm{Z}_{o}
is identical, and equal to an
equiproportional mixture of (normalized) isotropic Gaussians on the subspaces

\bm{U}_{1},\dots\bm{U}_{k}
, convolved with the noise distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{d}\bm{I})
.
This marginal distribution was studied in
Section

B.2
, where it was shown that
when the perturbation level
\sigma^{2}\to 0
, the score function for this
marginal distribution approximately implements a projection operation onto the
nearest subspace
\bm{U}_{k}
.

Hence, we can connect compression, as implemented in the MSSA block of the

crate
architecture, to denoising in the uncorrelated tokens model by showing
that at similar local scales, and for suitable settings of the model parameters,
the compression operation implements a projection onto the low-dimensional
structure of the distribution, as well.

Compression operation.

The MSSA block of the
crate
architecture arises from taking an (approximate)
gradient step on the
R^{c}
term of the sparse rate reduction objective

Equation

15
. This term writes

R^{c}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\log\det\left(\bm{I}+\beta(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right),

(212)

where

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{n\varepsilon^{2}},

(213)

and
\varepsilon>0
is the quantization error.
Calculating the gradient, we have

\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R^{c}(\bm{Z}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z})^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}\right)^{-1}.

(214)

Minimizing the sparse rate reduction objective corresponds to taking a gradient
descent step on
R^{c}(\,\cdot\,\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})
. Performing this operation at the
observation from the uncorrelated tokens model
\bm{Z}_{o}
, the output can be
written as

\bm{Z}^{+}=\bm{Z}_{o}-\eta\nabla R^{c}(\bm{Z}_{o}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]}),

(215)

where
\eta>0
is the step size.

Main result on projection.

We will see shortly that the behavior of
the compression output
Equation

215
depends on the relative
scales of the perturbation about the low-dimensional structure
\sigma^{2}
and
the target quantization error
\varepsilon^{2}
.

Theorem 16
.

There are universal constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}>0
such that the following holds.
Suppose
15
holds, and moreover suppose that

\sigma\leq 1
and
C_{1}\beta\sigma\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
.
Then with probability at least
1-KC_{2}\bigl(e^{-C_{3}d}+e^{-C_{4}n/K}+n^{-2}\bigr)
,
it holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{Z}^{+}-\left[\left(\bm{\Delta}-\eta\mathcal{P}_{\bm{U}_{[K]}}(\beta\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast})\bm{\Pi}\right)+\frac{1+\beta^{-1}-\eta}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{X}_{\natural}\right]\right\rVert

(216)

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}K\eta\left(\sigma^{2}\beta^{2}+\sigma(1+\sqrt{n/d})+\sqrt{K}\beta\sigma^{2}(1+\sqrt{n/d})+\sqrt{n/d}\right).

(217)

Here,

\mathcal{P}_{\bm{U}_{[K]}}
implements a projection onto the relevant subspaces for
each token in the limiting case as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and is precisely defined
in
Equations

298
and
299
.

We give the proof of
Theorem

16
below. First, we make three remarks
on interpreting the result, our technical assumptions, and our analysis.

Remark 17
.

Theorem

16
admits the following interesting interpretation in
an asymptotic setting, where we can identify the leading-order behavior of
the gradient and confirm our hypothesis about the connection between
compression and score-following.
Choose
\eta=\beta^{-1}
, so that the guarantee in
Theorem

16

incurs some cancellation, and moreover delineate more precise dependencies
on the RHS of the guarantee:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{Z}^{+}-\left[\left(\bm{\Delta}-\mathcal{P}_{\bm{U}_{[K]}}(\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast})\bm{\Pi}\right)+\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{X}_{\natural}\right]\right\rVert

(218)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\frac{nK^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{d}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}d^{2}}{n^{2}K^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}+\sigma(1+\sqrt{n/d})+\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{n\sqrt{K}\varepsilon^{2}}(1+\sqrt{n/d})+\sqrt{n/d}\right)

(219)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim K^{3/2}\sigma^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{n\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{nK^{2}}{d}\left(\sigma+\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right)\varepsilon^{2},

(220)

where we used
15
, which implies
p=d/K
and

n/d\leq 1
.
We will check in due course whether we have satisfied the hypotheses of

Theorem

16
, so that this guarantee indeed applies.
To this end, we optimize this bound as a function of
\varepsilon>0
, since this is a
parameter of the compression model.
The optimal
\varepsilon
is straightforward to compute using calculus: it
satisfies

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\left.\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{n}\right/\frac{K^{2}n}{d}\left(\sigma+\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right)}

(221)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma d}{nK\sqrt{\sigma+\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}}},

(222)

and the value of the residual arising from
Theorem

16
with this
choice of
\varepsilon
is no larger than an absolute constant multiple of

K^{3/2}\sigma^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{K^{2}\sigma^{2}d}{n}\left(\frac{n\sigma}{d}+\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)^{3/2}\right)}=K\sigma\left(\sqrt{K}\sigma+\sqrt{\sigma+\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}}\right).

(223)

Moreover, with this choice of
\varepsilon
,
\beta
satisfies

\beta^{-1}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}nK}{d}=\sqrt{\frac{\sigma}{1+\sqrt{\frac{n}{d\sigma^{2}}}}}.

(224)

In particular, the condition
\beta\sigma\lesssim 1
in

Theorem

16
demands

\sqrt{\sigma+\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}}\lesssim 1,

(225)

which holds for sufficiently small
\sigma
and sufficiently large
d\geq n
, showing that
Theorem

16
can be nontrivially applied in this
setting.
If we consider a simplifying limiting regime where
n,d\to+\infty
such
that
n/d\to 0
and
n/K\to+\infty
, we observe the following asymptotic
behavior of the guarantee of
Theorem

16
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{Z}^{+}-\left[\left(\bm{\Delta}-\mathcal{P}_{\bm{U}_{[K]}}(\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast})\bm{\Pi}\right)+\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{\sigma}}\bm{X}_{\natural}\right]\right\rVert\lesssim K\sigma^{3/2}\left(1+\sqrt{K\sigma}\right).

(226)

This demonstrates that a gradient step on
R^{c}
performs denoising: there is
a noise-level-dependent shrinkage effect applied to the signal

\bm{X}_{\natural}
, which vanishes as
\sigma\to 0
, and meanwhile the noise
term
\bm{\Delta}
is reduced.

Moreover, as
\sigma\to 0
, we can express the limiting form of

\mathcal{P}_{\bm{U}_{[K]}}
exactly as an orthogonal projection, since this drives

\beta^{-1}\to 0
:
following
Equations

298
and
299
, we have here

\mathcal{P}_{U_{[K]}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{P}_{1}&\ldots&\mathcal{P}_{K}\end{bmatrix},

(227)

where

\mathcal{P}_{k}\to\sum_{k^{\prime}\neq k}\bm{U}_{k^{\prime}}\operatorname{proj}_{\operatorname{im}(\bm{A}_{k^{\prime}})^{\perp}}\bm{U}_{k^{\prime}}^{\ast}.

(228)

This shows that, in an asymptotic sense, a gradient step on
R^{c}
serves to suppress
the effect of the perturbation applied to the observations
\bm{Z}_{o}

about the local signal model
\bm{X}_{\natural}
. This verifies our claim
previously that in this setting, there is a correspondence between a
score-following algorithm and a compression-based approach: locally, both
project the observations onto the structures of the signal model.

It can be shown moreover that the shrinkage effect on
\bm{X}_{\natural}

demonstrated here appears as a consequence of using the
R^{c}

“compression” term for the gradient step in
crate
; when the gradient
step is taken instead on the full
\Delta R
rate reduction objective (which
is computationally prohibitive, of course), there is zero shrinkage, and
perfect denoising is performed for a wider variety of step sizes
\eta
than the choice made here. We see the introduction of this shrinkage
effect this as the price of constructing an efficient and interpretable
network architecture. In practice, the ISTA block of
crate
counteracts
this shrinkage effect, which is anyways minor at reasonable parameter scales.

Remark 18
.

We have made two assumptions which may not hold exactly in practice: namely, we have assumed that the
\bm{U}_{k}
’s have orthogonal columns, namely
\bm{U}_{k}^{\top}\bm{U}_{k^{\prime}}={\mathds{1}}_{k=k^{\prime}}\bm{I}
, and we have assumed that the linear combination coefficients
\bm{A}_{k}
that form the matrix
\bm{X}_{\natural}
are i.i.d. samples from Gaussian distributions. Both these assumptions can be made more realistic, at the cost of additional (non-instructive) complexity in the analysis; we briefly go over how.

Relaxing the orthogonality condition
\bm{U}_{k}^{\top}\bm{U}_{k^{\prime}}={\mathds{1}}_{k=k^{\prime}}\bm{I}
to near-orthogonality, namely
\lVert\bm{U}_{k}^{\top}\bm{U}_{k^{\prime}}-{\mathds{1}}_{k=k^{\prime}}\bm{I}\rVert\leq\nu
for a small
\nu
, as observed in practice (recall
Figure

17
) would introduce additional small error terms in the proof, say polynomial in
\nu
. The magnitudes of these errors could in principle be precisely tracked, whence one could obtain a similar result to
Theorem

16
.

Secondly, we have assumed that the
\bm{A}_{k}
’s have independent columns which are sampled from (the same) Gaussian distribution. However, in the conceptual framework for
crate
, we exploit the joint distribution (and in particular the correlations) between the tokens in order to obtain good performance for our model. Our analysis is not completely agnostic to this fact; as we will see, the proof of
Theorem

16
only leverages the independence of the columns of each
\bm{A}_{k}
’s in order to obtain high-probability upper bounds on the smallest and largest singular value of the token matrices. If these bounds were ensured by some other method, such as appropriate normalization and incoherence, a similar conclusion to
Theorem

16
could hold in the more realistic correlated tokens model. Going beyond well-conditioned token matrices for each subspace would require additional modeling assumptions, and additional investigative experimental work to determine a realistic basis for such assumptions.

Remark 19
.

We have not attempted to optimize constants or rates of concentration in the
proof of
Theorem

16
, preferring instead to pursue a
straightforward analysis that leads to a qualitative interpretation of the
behavior of the rate reduction gradient in our model problem. Minor
improvements to the concentration analysis would enable the parameter
scaling requirements in
15
to be relaxed slightly,
and the probability bound in
Theorem

16
that scales as
K/n^{2}
can easily be improved to any positive power of
1/n
.

{proof}

[Proof of
Theorem

16
]
We start by noticing that, by orthonormality of the subspaces
\bm{U}_{k}
,
we have by
Equation

211

\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{\Pi}+\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta},

(229)

so that

\small\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o})^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\right)^{-1}=\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\left(\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\beta^{-1}\bm{I}&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&\\
&&\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}&&\\
&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&\beta^{-1}\bm{I}\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{\bm{D}_{k}}+\bm{\Xi}_{k}\right)^{-1}\bm{\Pi},

(230)

because permutation matrices are orthogonal matrices, and where the
perturbation
\bm{\Xi}_{k}
is defined by

\bm{\Xi}_{k}=\bm{\Pi}\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\\
\vdots&&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{K}\\
\vdots&&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\\
\end{bmatrix},

(231)

and where we have defined (implicitly) in addition

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}.

(232)

The matrix
\bm{D}_{k}\succ\mathbf{0}
, so we can write

\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o})^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\right)^{-1}=\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\left(\bm{I}+\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\bm{\Pi},

(233)

from which it follows

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o})^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\right)^{-1}

(234)

\displaystyle\quad=\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}+\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right)\left(\bm{I}+\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\bm{\Pi}.

(235)

The task before us is therefore to control
\lVert\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\rVert<1
, in order to apply the Neumann series to further simplify this
expression. We will do this in stages: first, we invoke several
auxiliary lemmas to construct a high-probability event on which the
random quantities in the preceding expression are controlled about their
nominal values; next, we show that the Neumann series can be applied on
this event and a main term extracted; finally, we simplify this main
term further in order to establish the claimed expression.

High-probability event construction.

In order to achieve the appropriate control on all random quantities, we would like to construct a high-probability event on which the random quantities are not too large. By
Propositions

22
,
23
and
24
and union bound, there exist universal constants
C_{i}>0
for which

\mathbb{P}\left[\begin{array}[]{rl}&\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert\leq\sigma(C_{1}+\sqrt{n/d})\\
\forall k\in[K]\colon&\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert\leq 1+C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}\\
\forall k\in[K]\colon&\lVert\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{I}\rVert\leq C_{3}\sqrt{n/d}\end{array}\right]\geq 1-C_{4}K(e^{-C_{5}d}+e^{-C_{6}n/K}+n^{-2}).

The event we compute the probability of, which we denote
E^{\star}
, is precisely the good event that we want. Formally,

E^{\star}\doteq\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}&\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert\leq\sigma(C_{1}+\sqrt{n/d})\\
\forall k\in[K]\colon&\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert\leq 1+C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}\\
\forall k\in[K]\colon&\lVert\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{I}\rVert\leq C_{3}\sqrt{n/d}\end{array}\right\}.

We know that
E^{\star}
occurs with high probability, and are able to strongly control the random quantities to the degree desired.

Main term extraction.

By
Lemma

21
and our hypotheses on the problem parameters, we have on
E^{\star}
that

\lVert\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\rVert\leq C\beta\sigma<1.

(244)

We can therefore apply the Neumann series to obtain

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o})^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\right)^{-1}

(245)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.8}{$\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}+\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right)\left(\bm{I}-\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\left(\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right)^{j}\right)\bm{\Pi}$}.

(246)

Again on
E^{\star}
, we have

\left\lVert\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\left(\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right)^{j}\right\rVert\leq\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\left\lVert\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert^{j}\leq C(\beta\sigma)^{2}\frac{1}{1-C\beta\sigma}\leq C^{\prime}(\beta\sigma)^{2}.

(247)

Moreover, as in the proof of
Lemma

21
, we have on the previous event
that

\left\lVert\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert\leq C\beta\sigma.

(248)

Thus, if we define a “main term”

\bm{M}_{k}=\left[\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\left(\bm{I}-\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right)+\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right]\bm{\Pi},

(249)

we have on the same event as previously

\left\lVert\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o})^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\right)^{-1}-\bm{M}_{k}\right\rVert\leq C(\beta\sigma)^{2}.

(250)

To conclude, we need only study this main term, since
\bm{U}_{k}
has operator norm
1
.

Simplifying the main term.

Our approach will be to control the main term
\bm{M}_{k}
around a simpler
expression, using basic perturbation theory; by the triangle inequality
for the operator norm, this will give control of the desired gradient
term.
After distributing,
\bm{M}_{k}
is a sum of three terms; we will start with
the simplest term. We first compute

\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}=\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots\bm{\Delta}_{k}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}\right)^{-1}&\ldots&\beta\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}.

(251)

We are going to argue that the residual

\left\lVert\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}-\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\beta\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert

(252)

is small. To this end, note that by the fact that
\bm{U}_{k}
has unit
operator norm,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}-\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\beta\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert

(253)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{k}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}\right)^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert

(254)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}\right)^{-1}\right\rVert

(255)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right\rVert\left\lVert\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}\right)^{-1}\right\rVert.

(256)

By
Section

B.3
and
Equation

302
from

Lemma

20
, the second term
here is controlled on
E^{\star}
.
For the first term, we note that by definition and the fact that
the unit sphere is invariant to rotations (and permutations are
rotations),
