\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}\right\rVert=\sup_{\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{2}\leq 1}\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}\bm{u}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{2}\leq 1}\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\bm{u}\right\rVert_{2}

(257)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{2}\leq 1}\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{K}\bm{\Delta}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\right\rVert_{2},

(258)

where
\bm{u}_{i}
are coordinate-subset-induced partitions of the vector

\bm{u}
induced by those of
\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}
.
This yields immediately

\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}\right\rVert\leq\sup_{\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{2}\leq 1}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left(\max_{k\in[K]}\,\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}\rVert\right)\sup_{\lVert\bm{u}\rVert_{2}\leq 1}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\lVert\bm{u}_{i}\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{K}\left(\max_{k\in[K]}\,\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}\rVert\right),

(259)

by the triangle inequality and inequalities for
\ell^{p}
norms.
Similarly, choosing a specific
\bm{u}
in the operator norm expression,
namely one that is supported entirely on one of the coordinate
partitions
\bm{u}_{i}
, shows that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}

(260)

for any
i
, whence

\max_{k\in[K]}\,\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}\rVert\leq\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}\right\rVert.

(261)

It follows that we control the first term above on
E^{\star}
.
Combining this reasoning, we conclude from the above

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}-\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\beta\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert

(262)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma(C+\sqrt{n/d})\left(\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}+\frac{C^{\prime}\sqrt{n/d}}{1+\beta^{-1}}\right)

(263)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sigma(1+C\sqrt{n/d}),

(264)

where the second line uses
15
to
remove the higher-order residual.

next, we recall that
\bm{\Xi}_{k}
is a sum of two terms; we will do one term
at a time for concision. We have first

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\bm{\Pi}\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}

(265)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Delta}_{1}^{\ast}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{\Delta}_{K}^{\ast}\end{bmatrix}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Delta}_{1}^{\ast}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{\Delta}_{K}^{\ast}\end{bmatrix}^{\ast}

(266)

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}.

(267)

We then multiply this term by
\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}
on the right to get the term
that appears in
\bm{M}_{k}
(ignoring the multiplication on the right by

\bm{\Pi}
, because it does not change operator norms).
In particular, we will control

\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert,

(268)

showing that this term is small.
We will accomplish this with the block diagonal structure of
\bm{D}_{k}
:
indeed, this gives that
\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}
is obtained by blockwise inversion,
and hence

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert

(269)

\displaystyle\quad=\scalebox{0.9}{$\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{k}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}\right)^{-1}&\ldots&\beta\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert$}

(270)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sqrt{K}\max\Bigl\{\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\right\rVert,

(271)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\max_{k^{\prime}\neq k}\,\beta\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k^{\prime}}\right\rVert\Bigr\},

(272)

where the second line uses
Equation

259
.
We will give a coarse control of this term—the error could be improved
further by exploiting more thoroughly independence of the blocks

\bm{\Delta}_{k}
to show that the maximum over
k^{\prime}\neq k
in the last line
of the preceding expression is smaller.
We have by submultiplicativity of the operator norm
and the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\right\rVert

(273)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}+\frac{C\sqrt{n/d}}{1+\beta^{-1}}\right)^{2}\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right\rVert

(274)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(1+C\sqrt{n/d}\right)\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right\rVert,

(275)

where the first line uses
Lemma

20
, and the second line
uses
15
to simplify the residual as above.
We have meanwhile
from the definition of
E^{\star}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right\rVert^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{2}\left(1+\sqrt{n/d}\right),

(276)

because
\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}
is an orthogonal projection, and again using

15
to simplify the residual.
We can argue analogously to simplify the other term in the maximum
appearing above, and this yields

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert

(277)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\sqrt{K}\beta\sigma^{2}\left(1+C\sqrt{n/d}\right)\left(1+\sqrt{n/d}\right),

(278)

where we used the fact that
\varepsilon\leq 1
and the rest of

15
implies that
\beta\geq 1
.
This residual simplifies using
15
to

\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert\lesssim\sqrt{K}\beta\sigma^{2}\left(1+C\sqrt{n/d}\right).

(279)

next, we examine the last term, which is the other summand arising in
the definition of
\bm{\Xi}_{k}
. We have

\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{1}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\\
\vdots&&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{K}\\
\vdots&&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{\Delta}_{K}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\\
\end{bmatrix}

(280)

\displaystyle=\scalebox{0.95}{$\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\left(\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right)&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}$}.

(281)

Now multiplying on the right by
\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}
gives the term (again
ignoring the right-multiplication by
\bm{\Pi}
, which does not affect the
operator norm)

\scalebox{0.8}{$\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\left(\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right)\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}\right)^{-1}&\ldots&\beta\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}$}.

(282)

We will argue that this term is close to the term

\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\beta\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}

(283)

in operator norm. The argument is similar to the preceding arguments:
for this, it suffices to bound

\scalebox{0.9}{$\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\left(\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right)\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}\right)^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert$},

(284)

which is the same as controlling the operator norm of the nonzero block.
Using submultiplicativity and
Lemma

20
along with the
simplifications we have done above leveraging

15
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\left(\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right)\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}\right)^{-1}\right\rVert

(285)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left(1+C\sqrt{n/d}\right)\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right\rVert.

(286)

Meanwhile, on
E^{\star}
we have the operator norm of
\bm{\Delta}_{k}
and

\bm{A}_{k}
controlled, using again

Equation

261
. Applying then
the triangle inequality and submultiplicativity, we obtain

\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}_{k}\right\rVert\lesssim\sigma\left(1+\sqrt{n/d}\right),

(287)

again simplifying with
15
.
This shows that
Equation

282
is close to

Equation

283

with deviations of the order
\lesssim\sigma(1+\sqrt{n/d})
.

Aggregating the previous results.

Combining our perturbation analysis above, we have established control

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\bm{M}_{k}-\Bigl[\left(\bm{I}-\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\right)\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\beta\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}

(288)

\displaystyle\quad+\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\Bigr]\bm{\Pi}\Bigr\|

(289)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma(1+\sqrt{n/d})+\sqrt{K}\beta\sigma^{2}(1+\sqrt{n/d}).

(290)

It is convenient to include one additional stage of simplification here:
namely, we use
Lemma

20
once more to simplify the
second term in the nominal value of
\bm{M}_{k}
appearing here.
namely, we have (arguing as we have above, once again)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert

(291)

\displaystyle\quad=\left\lVert\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{A}_{k}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k}\right)^{-1}\right\rVert

(292)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\sqrt{n/d},

(293)

from which it follows

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\bm{M}_{k}-\Bigl[\left(\bm{I}-\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\bm{A}_{k}^{\ast}\right)\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{\Delta}_{1}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\beta\bm{\Delta}_{K}\end{bmatrix}

(294)

\displaystyle\quad+\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\Bigr]\bm{\Pi}\Bigr\|

(295)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma(1+\sqrt{n/d})+\sqrt{K}\beta\sigma^{2}(1+\sqrt{n/d})+\sqrt{n/d}.

(296)

Meanwhile, recall the residual of scale
\lesssim(\sigma\beta)^{2}

arising when we controlled the gradient term around
\bm{M}_{k}
:

\left\lVert\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\left(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+(\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o})^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{Z}_{o}\right)^{-1}-\bm{M}_{k}\right\rVert\leq C(\beta\sigma)^{2}.

(297)

Combining these two bounds with the triangle inequality controls the
gradient term around its nominal value. Now, we sum these errors over

k
(again with the triangle inequality) to obtain control of the
aggregate gradient around its nominal value.
We introduce notation to concisely capture the
accumulations of the (approximate) orthogonal projections arising in the
nominal value of the main term: for each
k\in[K]
, define

\mathcal{P}_{k}=\sum_{k^{\prime}\neq k}\bm{U}_{k^{\prime}}\left(\bm{I}-\bm{A}_{k^{\prime}}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k^{\prime}}^{\ast}\bm{A}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}\bm{A}_{k^{\prime}}^{\ast}\right)\bm{U}_{k^{\prime}}^{\ast},

(298)

and define an overall (approximate) projection operator (which acts on block matrices
partitioned compatibly with the class sizes
n_{k}
, as in

Equation

211
) by

\mathcal{P}_{U_{[K]}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{P}_{1}&\ldots&\mathcal{P}_{K}\end{bmatrix}.

(299)

Then the above argument implies

\displaystyle\;\left\lVert\nabla_{\bm{Z}}R^{c}(\bm{Z}_{o}\mid\bm{U}_{[K]})-\mathcal{P}_{\bm{U}_{[K]}}(\beta\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast})\bm{\Pi}-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{X}_{\natural}\right\rVert

(300)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\;K\left(\sigma^{2}\beta^{2}+\sigma(1+\sqrt{n/d})+\sqrt{K}\beta\sigma^{2}(1+\sqrt{n/d})+\sqrt{n/d}\right),

(301)

which is enough to conclude.

B.3.1
Key Auxiliary Lemmas

In this section we state and prove two key concentration inequalities that are used in the proof of the main theorem. They rely on simpler results which will be conveyed in subsequent subsections.

Lemma 20
.

There exist universal constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
such that the following holds. Let
d,p,n,K\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
15
holds. Let
\bm{A}_{k}
,
k\in[K]
, be defined as above. Let
E^{\star}
be the good event defined in
Section

B.3
. If
E^{\star}
occurs, then for
k\in[K]
we have

\left\lVert(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{I}\right\rVert\leq\frac{C\sqrt{n/d}}{(1+\beta^{-1})}.

(302)

and in addition

\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{A}_{k}\right\rVert\leq\frac{C^{\prime}\sqrt{n/d}}{(1+\beta^{-1})}.

(303)

{proof}

Since
E^{\star}
holds, for all
k\in[K]
we have

\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert\leq 1+C_{1}\sqrt{n/d},\qquad\lVert\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{I}\rVert\leq C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}.

(304)

By
15
, we have
\lVert\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{I}\rVert<1
, so
\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}
is well-conditioned. Write

\bm{\Xi}\doteq\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{I},

(305)

so that

\displaystyle(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}

\displaystyle=((1+\beta^{-1})\bm{I}+\bm{\Xi})^{-1}

(306)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\left(\bm{I}+\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{\Xi}\right)^{-1}

(307)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\right)^{j}\bm{\Xi}^{j}

(308)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{I}+\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\right)^{j}\bm{\Xi}^{j}

(309)

by the Neumann series. This gives us

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{I}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\right)^{j}\bm{\Xi}^{j}\right\rVert

(310)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\right)^{j}\lVert\bm{\Xi}\rVert^{j}

(311)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}}{1+\beta^{-1}}\right)^{j}

(312)

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}}{(1+\beta^{-1})(1+\beta^{-1}-C_{2}\sqrt{n/d})}.

(313)

Meanwhile, by
15
, it holds

\displaystyle C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}\leq\sqrt{1/6},

(314)

so it follows

\frac{C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}}{1+\beta^{-1}-C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}}\leq 2C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}.

(315)

By the submultiplicativity of the operator norm, we thus have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{A}_{k}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert\left\lVert(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{I}\right\rVert

(316)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{[1+C_{1}\sqrt{n/d}]C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}}{(1+\beta^{-1})(1+\beta^{-1}-C_{2}\sqrt{n/d})}

(317)

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{[1+C_{1}\sqrt{n/d}]C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}}{1+\beta^{-1}}

(318)

\displaystyle=2\frac{C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}+C_{1}C_{2}n/d}{1+\beta^{-1}}.

(319)

By
15
, we have that there exists some absolute constant
C_{3}>0
with
C_{3}\cdot n/d\leq\sqrt{n/d}
, which gives

\left\lVert\bm{A}_{k}(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}-\frac{1}{1+\beta^{-1}}\bm{A}_{k}\right\rVert\leq 2\frac{(C_{2}+C_{1}C_{2}C_{3}^{-1})\sqrt{n/d}}{1+\beta^{-1}},

(320)

as desired.

Lemma 21
.

There exist universal constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that the following holds. Let
d,p,n,K\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
15
holds. Let
\bm{A}_{k}
,
k\in[K]
, be defined as above. Let
\bm{D}_{k}
be defined as in
Equation

230
. Let
\bm{\Xi}_{k}
be defined as in
Equation

231
. Let
E^{\star}
be the good event defined in
Section

B.3
. If
E^{\star}
occurs, then for
k\in[K]
we have

\lVert\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}\rVert^{-1}\leq C_{1}\beta[\sigma^{2}+\sigma(C_{2}+\sqrt{n/d})].

(321)

{proof}

Since we have

\bm{D}_{k}=\begin{bmatrix}\beta^{-1}\bm{I}&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&\\
&&\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}&&\\
&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&\beta^{-1}\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}

(322)

it holds that

\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\beta\bm{I}&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&\\
&&(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}&&\\
&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&\beta\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}.

(323)

We will use the straightforward estimate
\lVert\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\rVert\leq\lVert\bm{\Xi}_{k}\rVert\lVert\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\rVert
and bound the two matrices’ operator norms individually. By the previous expression,

\lVert\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\rVert=\max\{\beta,\lVert(\beta^{-1}\bm{I}+\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k})^{-1}\rVert\}\leq\beta,

(324)

because
\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}\succeq\mathbf{0}
, so we need only control the operator norm of
\bm{\Xi}_{k}
.
To this end, note the convenient expression

\bm{\Xi}_{k}=\bm{\Pi}\bm{\Delta}^{\ast}\bm{U}_{k}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast}+2\operatorname{sym}\left((\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast})^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\right),

(325)

where
\operatorname{sym}(\,\cdot\,)
denotes the symmetric part operator.
By the triangle inequality, the operator norm of
\bm{\Xi}_{k}
is no larger
than the sum of the operator norms of each term in the previous
expression.
The operator norm of the first term is no larger than

\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert^{2}
, because
\bm{\Pi}
is a permutation matrix and
\bm{U}\bm{U}_{k}^{\ast}
is an orthogonal projection.
Meanwhile, using that the symmetric part operator is the orthogonal
projection onto the space of symmetric matrices, it follows

\scalebox{0.9}{$\left\lVert 2\operatorname{sym}\left((\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast})^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right\rVert\leq 2\left\lVert(\bm{\Delta}\bm{\Pi}^{\ast})^{\ast}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\ldots&\bm{U}_{k}\bm{A}_{k}&\ldots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert$},

(326)

and then we find as above that the RHS is no larger than
2\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert
. Since the good event
E^{\star}
defined in
Section

B.3
holds by assumption, we have that there are constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\left(C_{1}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right)

(327)

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 1+C_{2}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}.

(328)

By
15
we have
d\geq n
, so that
\sqrt{n/d}\leq 1
. Therefore we have

\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert\leq\sigma\left(C_{1}+1\right)=C_{3}\sigma

(329)

for
C_{3}\doteq C_{1}+1
another universal constant. Thus on this good event we have

2\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert\leq C_{3}\sigma\left(1+C_{2}\sqrt{n/d}\right).

(330)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Xi}_{k}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert^{2}+2\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert

(331)

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}^{2}\sigma^{2}+C_{3}\sigma(1+C_{2}\sqrt{n/d})

(332)

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}[\sigma^{2}+\sigma(1+C_{2}\sqrt{n/d})]

(333)

where
C_{4}=\max\{C_{3},C_{3}^{2}\}
is another universal constant. Thus on
E^{\star}
we have

\lVert\bm{\Xi}_{k}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\rVert\leq C_{4}\beta[\sigma^{2}+\sigma(1+C_{2}\sqrt{n/d})]\leq C_{5}\beta[\sigma^{2}+\sigma(1+\sqrt{n/d})]

(334)

for
C_{5}>0
another obvious universal constant.

B.3.2
Concentration Inequalities for Our Setting

In this section we prove some simple concentration inequalities that are adapted to the problem setting. These results are used to prove the key lemmata above, and indeed are also invoked in the main theorem. They follow from even simpler concentration inequalities that are abstracted away from the problem setting, which we discuss in the following subsections.

Proposition 22
.

There are universal constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
such that the following holds. Let
d,n\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
15
holds. Let
\bm{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
be defined as above. Then

\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lVert\bm{\Delta}\right\rVert>\sigma\left(C_{1}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right)\right]\leq C_{2}e^{-C_{3}d}.

(335)

{proof}

We use
Proposition

27
with the parameters
q=d
,
n=n
, and
x=\sigma/\sqrt{d}
, which obtains

\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert>s]\leq C_{1}\exp\left(-d\left\{\frac{s\sqrt{d}/\sigma-\sqrt{n}}{C_{2}\sqrt{d}}-1\right\}^{2}\right),\qquad\forall s>\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{d}}(\sqrt{n}+C_{2}\sqrt{d})

(336)

notice that we have

\frac{s\sqrt{d}/\sigma-\sqrt{n}}{C_{2}\sqrt{d}}-1=\frac{1}{C_{2}}\left(\frac{s}{\sigma}-\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right)-1,\qquad\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{d}}(\sqrt{n}+C_{2}\sqrt{d})=\sigma\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}+C_{2}\right).

(337)

To make the squared term equal to
1
, we pick

s=\sigma\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}+2C_{2}\right),

(338)

which gives

\mathbb{P}\left[\lVert\bm{\Delta}\rVert>\sigma\left(2C_{2}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right)\right]\leq C_{2}e^{-d}.

(339)

Proposition 23
.

There are universal constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}>0
such that the following holds. Let
p,n,K\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
15
holds. Let
\bm{A}_{k}
,
k\in[K]
, be defined as above. Then

\mathbb{P}\left[\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert>1+C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right]\leq C_{2}\exp\left(-C_{3}\frac{n}{K}\right)+\frac{C_{4}}{n^{2}}

(340)

{proof}

By
Propositions

25
and
27
with parameters
n=n
,
k=K
,
q=p
, and
x=1/\sqrt{p}
, if we define

n_{\min}\doteq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{K}-C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right\rfloor,\qquad n_{\max}\doteq\left\lceil\frac{n}{K}+C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right\rceil

(341)

then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert>s]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert>s\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}K_{k}=n]\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(342)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}C_{3}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{s\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{p}}{C_{4}\sqrt{4}}-1\right\}^{2}\right)\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}},

(343)

for all
s
obeying

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\left(\sqrt{p}+C_{4}\sqrt{n_{\max}}\right)

(344)

\displaystyle=1+C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{n_{\max}}{p}}.

(345)

Thus we have that the concentration holds for all
s
obeying

s\geq 1+C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{n_{\max}}{p}}.

(346)

In order to cancel out the most interior terms, we choose

s=1+2C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{n_{\max}}{p}}.

(347)

This choice obtains

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{A}_{k}\rVert>s]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}C_{3}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{s\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{p}}{C_{4}\sqrt{n}}-1\right\}^{2}\right)\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(348)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}C_{3}\exp\left(-n\underbrace{\left\{2\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{n_{\max}}{n}}}_{\geq 1}-1\right\}^{2}}_{\geq 1}\right)\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(349)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}C_{3}\exp\left(-n\right)\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(350)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}C_{3}\exp\left(-n_{\min}\right)\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(351)

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\exp(-n_{\min})+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(352)

\displaystyle=C_{3}\exp\left(-\frac{n}{K}+C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right)+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(353)

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\exp\left(-\frac{n}{K}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{n\log n}\right)+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(354)

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{n}{K}\right)+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}.

(355)

To obtain the conclusion of the theorem, note that any
s
such that

s\geq 1+2C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{n_{\max}}{p}}=1+2C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{n/K}{p}+C_{1}\frac{\sqrt{n\log n}}{p}}=1+2C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}+C_{1}\frac{\sqrt{n\log n}}{p}}

(356)

enjoys the same high-probability bound. By
15
, we have

\displaystyle\;1+2C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}+C_{1}\frac{\sqrt{n\log n}}{p}}\leq 1+2C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{n/K}{p}}

(357)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1+2C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{n}{d}}=1+2C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}

(358)

whence the ultimate conclusion is obtained by combining constants.

Proposition 24
.

There are universal constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}>0
such that the following holds. Let
p,n,K\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
15
holds. Let
\bm{A}_{k}
,
k\in[K]
, be defined as above. Then

\mathbb{P}\left[\lVert\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{I}\rVert>C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right]\leq C_{2}\exp\left(-C_{3}\frac{n}{K}\right)+\frac{C_{4}}{n^{2}}.

(359)

{proof}

By
Propositions

25
and
28
with parameters
n=n
,
k=K
,
q=p
, and
x=1/\sqrt{p}
, if we define

n_{\min}\doteq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{K}-C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right\rfloor,\qquad n_{\max}\doteq\left\lceil\frac{n}{K}+C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right\rceil

(360)

then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{I}\rVert>s]

(361)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{I}\rVert>s\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}K_{k}=n]\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(362)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}+\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]\cdot\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{cases}C_{3}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{1}{C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{n/p}}s-1\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{n/p}\leq s\leq C_{4}^{2}\\
C_{3}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{1}{C_{4}C_{5}\sqrt{n/p}}\sqrt{s}-1\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ s\geq C_{4}^{2}.\end{cases}$}

(363)

In order to cancel the most terms, we choose

s=2C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{\frac{n_{\max}}{p}}.

(364)

In order to assure ourselves that this choice still has
s\leq C_{4}^{2}
(so that we can use the first case for all
n
), we have

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=2C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{\frac{n_{\max}}{p}}

(365)

\displaystyle=2C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{\frac{n/K+C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}}{p}}

(366)

\displaystyle=2C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{\frac{n/K+\frac{1}{2}n/K}{p}}

(367)

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n/K}{p}}

(368)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{6}C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}

(369)

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}^{2}\quad\text{when}\quad\sqrt{6}C_{5}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\leq 1.

(370)

Of course, this condition is assured by
15
. Now that we have this, we know
s
falls in the first, and so we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\lVert\bm{A}_{k}^{\top}\bm{A}_{k}-\bm{I}\rVert>C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}\right]

(371)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}+\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]\cdot\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{cases}C_{3}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{1}{C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{n/p}}s-1\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{n/p}\leq s\leq C_{4}^{2}\\
C_{3}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{1}{C_{4}C_{5}\sqrt{n/p}}\sqrt{s}-1\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ s\geq C_{4}^{2}\end{cases}$}

(372)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}+\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}C_{3}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{2C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{n_{\max}/p}}{C_{4}^{2}C_{5}\sqrt{n/p}}-1\right\}^{2}\right)\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]

(373)

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}+\sum_{n=n_{\min}}^{n_{\max}}C_{3}\exp\left(-n\left\{2\sqrt{\frac{n_{\max}}{n}}-1\right\}^{2}\right)\mathbb{P}[K_{k}=n]

(374)

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{n}{K}\right)+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}

(375)

where the last inequality follows from the exact same argument as in
Proposition

23
.

B.3.3
Generic Concentration Inequalities

In this subsection we prove the base-level concentration inequalities used throughout the proofs in this paper.

Binomial concentration.

Proposition 25
.

There exist universal constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that the following holds. Let
n,k\in\mathbb{Z}
. For each
i\in[k]
, let
B_{i}\sim\operatorname{Bin}(n,1/k)
, such that the
B_{i}
are identically (marginally) distributed but not necessarily independent binomial random variables. Let
E
be an event. Then for any
i\in[k]
, we have

\mathbb{P}[E]\leq\sum_{b=\left\lfloor n/k-C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right\rfloor}^{\left\lceil n/k+C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right\rceil}\mathbb{P}[E\;\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\;B_{i}=b]\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b]+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}.

(376)

{proof}

We have

\mathbb{P}[E]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}]]=\sum_{b=0}^{n}\mathbb{P}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}=b]\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b].

(377)

Each
B_{i}
is unconditionally distributed as
\operatorname{Bin}(n,1/k)
. By union bound and Hoeffding’s inequality
(
Vershynin 2018
, Theorem 2.2.6)
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\lvert B_{i}-n/k\rvert\geq t\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{2t^{2}}{n}\right).

(378)

Inverting this inequality obtains that there exists some (simple) universal constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left[\lvert B_{i}-n/k\rvert\geq C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right]\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n^{3}}.

(379)

Thus, if we define

b_{\min}\doteq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{k}-C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right\rfloor,\qquad b_{\max}\doteq\left\lceil\frac{n}{k}+C_{1}\sqrt{n\log n}\right\rceil,

(380)

then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[E]

\displaystyle=\sum_{b=0}^{n}\mathbb{P}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}=b]\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b]

(381)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b=0}^{b_{\min}-1}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}=b]}_{\leq 1}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b]}_{\leq C_{2}/n^{3}}+\sum_{b=b_{\max}+1}^{n}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}=b_{1}]}_{\leq 1}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b]}_{\leq C_{2}/n^{3}}

(382)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{b=b_{\min}}^{b_{\max}}\mathbb{P}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}=b]\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b]

(383)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b=0}^{b_{\min}-1}\frac{C_{2}}{n^{3}}+\sum_{b=b_{\max}+1}^{n}\frac{C_{2}}{n^{3}}+\sum_{b=b_{\min}}^{b_{\max}}\mathbb{P}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}=b]\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b]

(384)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b=0}^{n}\frac{C_{2}}{n^{3}}+\sum_{b=b_{\min}}^{b_{\max}}\mathbb{P}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}=b]\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b]

(385)

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{2}}{n^{2}}+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{3}}+\sum_{b=b_{\min}}^{b_{\max}}\mathbb{P}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}=b]\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b]

(386)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C_{2}}{n^{2}}+\sum_{b=b_{\min}}^{b_{\max}}\mathbb{P}[E\nonscript\>|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}B_{i}=b]\mathbb{P}[B_{i}=b].

(387)

Remark 26
.

Notice that a simple adaptation of this argument can turn the additive probability
C_{3}/n^{2}
into
C_{3}^{\prime}/n^{z}
for any positive integer
z\in\mathbb{N}
(where
C_{3}^{\prime}
depends on
z
). However, trying to replace it with
C_{3}^{\prime}e^{-C^{\prime}n}
is more difficult.

Gaussian concentration.

Proposition 27
.

There are universal constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
such that the following holds. Let
n,q\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\bm{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times n}
be such that
M_{ij}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{N}(0,x^{2})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{M}\rVert>s]

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{s/x-\sqrt{q}}{C_{2}\sqrt{n}}-1\right\}^{2}\right),\qquad\forall s>x\left\{\sqrt{q}+C_{2}\sqrt{n}\right\}

(388)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{M}\rVert>s]

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\exp\left(-q\left\{\frac{s/x-\sqrt{n}}{C_{3}\sqrt{q}}-1\right\}^{2}\right),\qquad\forall s>x\left\{\sqrt{n}+C_{3}\sqrt{q}\right\}.

(389)

{proof}

Define
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\doteq\frac{1}{x}\bm{M}
, so that
M_{ij}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. By
Vershynin 2018
, we see that each row
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{i}
has Orlicz norm
\lVert\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{i}\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leq C_{1}
for some universal constant
C_{1}>0
.

By
Vershynin 2018
we have for some other universal constant
C_{2}>0
that for all
t>0
,

\sqrt{q}-C_{1}^{2}C_{2}(\sqrt{n}+t)\leq\sigma_{\min(n,q)}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\leq\sigma_{1}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\leq\sqrt{q}+C_{1}^{2}C_{2}(\sqrt{n}+t)

(390)

with probability at least
1-2e^{-t^{2}}
. Defining
C_{3}\doteq C_{1}^{2}C_{2}
and noting that
\lVert\cdot\rVert=\sigma_{1}(\cdot)
, we have with the same probability that

\lVert\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\rVert-\sqrt{q}\leq C_{3}\left(\sqrt{n}+t\right).

(391)

Simplifying, we obtain

\displaystyle\lVert\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\rVert-\sqrt{q}

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\left(\sqrt{n}+t\right)

(392)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x}\lVert\bm{M}\rVert-\sqrt{q}

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\left(\sqrt{n}+t\right)

(393)

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{M}\rVert-x\sqrt{q}

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}x\left(\sqrt{n}+t\right)

(394)

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{M}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq x\left\{\sqrt{q}+C_{3}\left(\sqrt{n}+t\right)\right\}.

(395)

Define
s>0
by

s\doteq x\left\{\sqrt{q}+C_{3}\left(\sqrt{n}+t\right)\right\}\iff t=\frac{s/x-\sqrt{q}}{C_{3}}-\sqrt{n}.

(396)

note that the range of validity is

t>0\iff s>x\left\{\sqrt{q}+C_{3}\sqrt{n}\right\}.

(397)

For
s
in this range, we have

\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{M}\rVert>s]\leq 2\exp\left(-\left\{\frac{s/x-\sqrt{q}}{C_{3}}-\sqrt{n}\right\}^{2}\right)=2\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{s/x-\sqrt{q}}{C_{3}\sqrt{n}}-1\right\}^{2}\right).

(398)

The other inequality follows from applying this inequality to
\bm{M}^{\top}
.

Proposition 28
.

There are universal constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
such that the following holds. Let
n,q\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\bm{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times n}
be such that
M_{ij}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{N}(0,x^{2})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lVert\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{M}-qx^{2}\bm{I}\right\rVert>s\right]

(399)

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}C_{1}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{1}{C_{2}^{2}C_{3}\sqrt{nq}x^{2}}s-1\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ C_{2}^{2}C_{3}\sqrt{nq}x^{2}\leq s\leq C_{2}^{2}qx^{2}\\
C_{1}\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{1}{C_{2}C_{3}\sqrt{n}x}\sqrt{s}-1\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ s\geq C_{2}^{2}qx^{2}.\end{cases}

(400)

{proof}

Define
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\doteq\frac{1}{x}\bm{M}
, so that
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{ij}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. By
Vershynin 2018
, we see that each row has Orlicz norm
\lVert\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{i}\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leq C_{1}
for some universal constant
C_{1}>0
.

By
Vershynin 2018
we have for some other universal constant
C_{2}>0
that for all
t>0
,

\left\lVert\frac{1}{q}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\top}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu-\bm{I}\right\rVert\leq C_{1}^{2}\max\{\delta,\delta^{2}\}\quad\text{where}\quad\delta\doteq C_{2}\frac{\sqrt{n}+t}{\sqrt{q}}.

(401)

with probability at least
1-2e^{-t^{2}}
. Simplifying, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{1}{q}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\top}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu-\bm{I}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}^{2}\max\{\delta,\delta^{2}\}

(402)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\top}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu-q\bm{I}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}^{2}q\max\{\delta,\delta^{2}\}

(403)

\displaystyle\left\lVert(x^{-1}\bm{M})^{\top}(x^{-1}\bm{M})-q\bm{I}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}^{2}q\max\{\delta,\delta^{2}\}

(404)

\displaystyle\left\lVert x^{-2}\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{M}-q\bm{I}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}^{2}q\max\{\delta,\delta^{2}\}

(405)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{M}-qx^{2}\bm{I}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}^{2}qx^{2}\cdot\max\{\delta,\delta^{2}\}.

(406)

Now from simple algebra and the fact that
n\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\max\left\{\delta,\delta^{2}\right\}=\delta

\displaystyle\iff 0\leq t\leq C_{2}^{-1}\sqrt{q}-\sqrt{n}

(407)

\displaystyle\max\left\{\delta,\delta^{2}\right\}=\delta^{2}

\displaystyle\iff t\geq C_{2}^{-1}\sqrt{q}-\sqrt{n}.

(408)

Now define
s\geq 0
by

s\doteq C_{1}^{2}qx^{2}\cdot\max\{\delta,\delta^{2}\}.

(409)

Thus in the first case we have

s\doteq C_{1}^{2}C_{2}\sqrt{q}x^{2}(\sqrt{n}+t)\iff t=\frac{1}{C_{1}^{2}C_{2}\sqrt{q}x^{2}}s-\sqrt{n},

(410)

and in particular the first case holds when

\max\{\delta,\delta^{2}\}=\delta\iff 0\leq t\leq C_{2}^{-1}\sqrt{q}-\sqrt{n}\iff C_{1}^{2}C_{2}\sqrt{nq}x^{2}\leq s\leq C_{1}^{2}qx^{2}.

(411)

Meanwhile, in the second case, we have

s\doteq C_{1}^{2}C_{2}^{2}(\sqrt{n}+t)^{2}x^{2}\iff t=\frac{1}{C_{1}C_{2}x}\sqrt{s}-\sqrt{n}

(412)

where we obtain only one solution to the quadratic equation by requiring
t\geq 0
, and in particular the second case holds when

\max\{\delta,\delta^{2}\}=\delta\iff t\geq C_{2}^{-1}\sqrt{q}-\sqrt{n}\iff s\geq C_{1}^{2}qx^{2}.

(413)

Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{M}-qx^{2}\bm{I}\rVert>s]

(414)

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}2\exp\left(-\left\{\frac{1}{C_{1}^{2}C_{2}\sqrt{q}x^{2}}s-\sqrt{n}\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ C_{1}^{2}C_{2}\sqrt{nq}x^{2}\leq s\leq C_{1}^{2}qx^{2}\\
2\exp\left(-\left\{\frac{1}{C_{1}C_{2}x}\sqrt{s}-\sqrt{n}\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ s\geq C_{1}^{2}qx^{2}\end{cases}

(415)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{1}{C_{1}^{2}C_{2}\sqrt{nq}x^{2}}s-1\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ C_{1}^{2}C_{2}\sqrt{nq}x^{2}\leq s\leq C_{1}^{2}qx^{2}\\
2\exp\left(-n\left\{\frac{1}{C_{1}C_{2}\sqrt{n}x}\sqrt{s}-1\right\}^{2}\right),&\text{if}\ s\geq C_{1}^{2}qx^{2}.\end{cases}

(416)

B.4
Companion to
Section

3.3

In this section, we justify the scaling applied to
\nabla R^{c}
in
Section

3.2
, and supply the discretization scheme used in
Section

3.3
.

First, suppose that
\bm{Z}_{\natural}^{\ell}
satisfies
Equation

10
, and
\bm{Z}_{t}\doteq\bm{Z}_{\natural}^{\ell}+\sigma_{t}\bm{W}
, where
\bm{W}
is a standard Gaussian matrix, so that
\bm{Z}_{t}
satisfies
Equation

11
with noise level
\sigma_{t}>0
. Let
q_{t}
be the density of
\bm{Z}_{t}
. Theoretical analysis from
(
Lu et al. 2023
)
and empirical analysis from
(
Song and Ermon 2019
)
demonstrates that under generic conditions, we have that

\lVert\nabla q_{t}(\bm{Z}_{t})\rVert_{2}\propto\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}},

(417)

ignoring all terms in the right-hand side except for those involving
\sigma_{t}
. On the other hand, from the proof of
Theorem

16
, we obtain that
-\nabla R^{c}(\bm{Z}_{t})
has constant (in
\sigma_{t}
) magnitude with high probability. Thus, in order to have them be the same magnitude, we need to divide
-\nabla R^{c}(\bm{Z}_{t})
by
\sigma_{t}^{2}
to have it be a drop-in replacement for the score function, as alluded to in
Section

3.2
.

Second, we wish to explicitly state our discretization scheme given in
Section

3.3
. To wit, we provide a discretization scheme that turns the structured diffusion ODE
Equation

61
into its gradient descent analogue
Equation

62
; the other discretization, namely of the structured denoising ODE, from
Equation

55
to
Equation

56
occurs similarly. To begin with, define the shorthand notation

f(t,\widetilde{\bm{Z}}(t))\doteq\nabla R^{c}(\widetilde{\bm{Z}}(t)\mid\bm{U}_{[K]}(T-t)),

(418)

so that we have

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\bm{Z}}(t)=\frac{1}{2t}f(t,\widetilde{\bm{Z}}(t))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

(
61
)

Fix
L
, and let
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{L}=T
, such that
t_{1}
is small. (These will be specified shortly in order to supply the discretization scheme.) A suitable first-order discretization is given by

\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\ell+1}\approx\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\ell}+\frac{t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}}{2t_{\ell}}f(t_{\ell},\widetilde{\bm{Z}}^{\ell}).

(419)

Thus it remains to set
t_{1},\dots,t_{L}
such that

\frac{t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}}{2t_{\ell}}=\kappa

(420)

for some constant
\kappa
, we observe that we must set

t_{\ell+1}=(1+2\kappa)t_{\ell},

(421)

so that the time grows exponentially in the index. The reverse process time decays exponentially in the index, which matches practical discretization schemes for ordinary diffusion models
(
Song and Ermon 2019
)
. Finally, we have
T=t_{L}=(1+2\kappa)^{L}t_{1}
, so that
t_{1}=\frac{T}{(1+2\kappa)^{L}}
.

Appendix C
Additional Implementation Details and Experimental Results

In this section, we provide details about our experiments, and report the results of additional experiments that were not covered in the main text.
crate
takes arguably the most basic design choices possible, and so we do
not
attempt to directly compete with state-of-the-art performance from heavily engineered and empirically designed transformers.
The results of our experiments are meant to convey a few core messages:

•

Despite not being engineered to compete with the state-of-the-art,
crate
performs strongly on large-scale real-world datasets
, including classification on ImageNet-1K.
crate
also achieves strong transfer learning performance.

•

Because our model is designed through unrolled optimization of a well-understood objective, each layer is interpretable
. In particular, we can analyze the performance of
crate
, as well as design network modifications, on a
layer-wise basis
. This is powered by an arguably unparalleled level of insight into the role of each operator in our network.

•

We make the simplest possible choices during the design of
crate
, but these can be changed easily while keeping the same framework
. We study a few modifications later in this section (
Section

C.5
) and show that they do not significantly hurt empirical performance, but emphasize here that there is significant potential for improvement with different architecture choices (and in particular a different theoretical analysis).

C.1
Details about CRATE for Image Classification

In this subsection, we provide more details for implementing
crate
on vision tasks.

Training setup.

We fine-tune our pre-trained
crate
on the following target datasets: CIFAR10/CIFAR100
(
Krizhevsky et al. 2009
)
, Oxford Flowers-102
(
Nilsback and Zisserman 2008
)
, Oxford-IIIT-Pets
(
Parkhi et al. 2012
)
.
For each fine-tuning task, we employ the AdamW optimizer
(
Loshchilov and Hutter 2019
)
.
We configure the learning rate as
5\times 10^{-5}
, weight decay as
0.01
, and batch size as
256
.
To allow transfer learning, in all training and evaluations setups we first resize our input data to 224 height and width. For data augmentations during pre-training and fine-tuning, we also adopt several standard techniques: random cropping, random horizontal flipping, and random augmentation (with number of transformations
n=2
and magnitude of transformations
m=14
).

Pre-training on ImageNet-1K.

We apply the Lion optimizer
(
Chen et al. 2023c
)
for pre-training both
crate
and ViT models.
We configure the learning rate as
2.4\times 10^{-4}
, weight decay as 0.5, and batch size as 2,048.
We incorporate a warm-up strategy with a linear increase over 5 epochs, followed by training the models for a total of 150 epochs with cosine decay.
For data augmentation, we only apply the standard techniques, random cropping and random horizontal flipping, on the ImageNet-1K dataset.
We apply label smoothing with smoothing parameter
0.1
.
One training epoch of
crate
-Base takes around 240 seconds using 16 A100 40GB GPUs.

Fine-tuning on image classification tasks.

For each fine-tuning task, we use the AdamW optimizer
(
Loshchilov and Hutter 2019
)
.
We configure the learning rate as
5\times 10^{-5}
, weight decay as 0.01, and batch size as 512.
To allow transfer learning, we first resize our input data to 224. For data augmentations, we also adopt several standard techniques: random cropping, random horizontal flipping, and random augmentation (with number of transformations
n=2
and magnitude of transformations
m=14
).
23
23

23

https://github.com/huggingface/pytorch-image-models/blob/main/timm/data/auto_augment.py

Pre-training on ImageNet-1K.

We apply the AdamW optimizer
(
Loshchilov and Hutter 2019
)
for pre-training both
crate
-MAE models on ImageNet-1K.
We configure the learning rate as
3\times 10^{-5}
, weight decay as
0.1
, and batch size as
4,096
.
We incorporate a warm-up strategy with a linear increase over 40 epochs, followed by training the models for a total of 800 epochs with cosine decay.
For data augmentation, we apply the standard augmentations as used in
He et al. 2021
, random cropping and random horizontal flipping.

Fine-tuning CRATE-MAE models.

Recall that in
Section

4.1.2
, we described two methods of fine-tuning our model: full fine-tuning, which updates all the weights, and linear probing via logistic regression, which only updates the classification head. For full fine-tuning, we apply the same fine-tuning configuration (data augmentation, training epochs, optimization, etc.) as described in
Section

C.1
. For linear probing, we apply the optimization solver in
scikit-learn
, i.e.,
linear_model.LogisticRegression
, to learn a logistic regression model with
\ell^{2}
regularization, and use cross-validation to select the
\ell^{2}
regularization parameter for each model-dataset pair. In our experiments, we consider
\ell^{2}
regularization parameters from the set
\{10^{-3},10^{-2},10^{-1},1,10,10^{2},10^{3}\}
.

C.3
Details about CRATE-DINO for Self-Supervised Learning

DINO pre-training.

To train
crate
with the DINO self-supervised learning objective, we use the ImageNet-1K dataset and match our training recipe closely to the official implementation with some minor variations. Specifically, we choose the base learning rate to be
0.001
and the weight decay parameter to be
0.005
in our experiments.
Other hyper-parameters such as the temperature parameters
\tau_{t}
and
\tau_{s}
are chosen to exactly match with the publicly available implementation of DINO
24
24

24

https://github.com/facebookresearch/dino
. Due to computational limitations, we only train our models for 100 epochs, compared to 400 to 800 epochs of training for the official implementation.

Linear probing models trained via DINO.

As introduced in
Section

4.1.3
, we use both weighted nearest neighbor (
k-
NN) and logistic regression on top of the features extracted from the teacher networks after pre-training with DINO. For
k-
NN, we search the number of neighbors
k
from the candidate set of
\{10,20,100,200\}
and choose the model that achieves the highest training accuracy. For logistic regression, we use the same procedure as introduced earlier for
crate
-MAE in
Section

C.2
.

Details about pre-training for CRATE-BERT.

We use a batch size of 8,096 and train for 30,000 steps with the Adam optimizer
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
.
For the Adam optimizer, we use
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0.9,0.98)
, and a weight decay of
0.01
.
For the learning rate scheduler, we apply the linear warm-up and linear decay, with the peak learning rate at the iteration of 1,800 steps with value
\eta=10^{-3}
.

Details about pre-training for CRATE-GPT.

We use a batch size of 384 and train for 600,000 steps with the Adam optimizer
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
.
For the Adam optimizer, we use
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0.9,0.95)
, and weight decay of
0.1
.
For the learning rate scheduler, we apply the linear warm-up and cosine decay, with a peak value of
\eta=6\times 10^{-4}
at the
2,000
iteration, and minimum value
6\times 10^{-5}
.
The training and validation losses over iterations are shown in Figure
12
(right).
The training/validation loss converges around
3.37
after training with a batch size of 384 and 600,000 iterations.
In comparison, the open GPT-2 implementation is pre-trained on OpenWebText with a batch size of 512 and 600,000 steps, and converges to a validation loss of
2.85

(
Karpathy 2022
)
.

Details about fine-tuning CRATE-BERT on GLUE.

For all the tasks in GLUE, we use a learning rate of
2\times 10^{-5}
without any hyperparameter sweep. We fine-tune the three models (BERT-Base, BERT-Medium and
crate
-BERT-Base) using the same fine-tuning configuration.
We fine-tune for 8 epochs on MNLI, for 5 epochs on WNLI and MRPC (because these two datasets are tiny), and for 3 epochs on all other tasks. For all tasks, we use a batch size of 32.
The maximum sequence length is set to 128 for WNLI and 512 for all other tasks. We find that the performance of BERT on WNLI is very sensitive to the sequence length, so we picked the sequence length to be the same as
Huggingface 2020
(while
crate
-BERT is very stable to the sequence length). We do not observe a significant difference of performance using different sequence length for other tasks.
Evaluations shown in Figure
12
are meant to demonstrate the effectiveness of learning, so we finetune 3 epochs for all tasks (except MNLI in the 24000 and 30000 step), and we use the sequence length of 512 for all tasks.

Details about CRATE-GPT evaluation.

We use the test split as the validation set for all tasks. The evaluation datasets WikiText2 and WikiText103 has the same test split so we merge them to WikiText. For both models (GPT2-Base and
crate
-GPT2-Base) on all tasks, we use a block size of
1,024
, evaluation batch size of
4
, and evaluate for
1,000
iterations to get a more accurate estimation.

Exploring architecture variants of CRATE.

In this section, we explore the two following alternative architectures. One architecture involves a modification to the attention mechanism, while the other involves a modification to the sparsification mechanism. Again, we re-emphasize that these choices, although principled, are entirely modular and the choices we make here still lead to very simple architectures. A more sophisticated analysis may lead to different, more complicated architectures that perform better in practice. The architectures we experiment with are:

•

Compression-inspired attention mechanism: revert the change in
Equation

37
. That is, the attention mechanism implements
Equations

35
and
36
directly.

•

Majorization-minimization proximal step sparsification: instead of
Equation

43
, implement
Equation

123
.

We obtain the following classification results in
Table

12
.
After conducting additional simplifications to the network architecture (i.e., imposing additional constraints to the network architecture design), we discover that
crate
maintains reasonable performance on ImageNet-1K.

Pre-training on ImageNet-21K.

We study
crate
when pre-training with supervised learning on a larger pre-training dataset, ImageNet-21K
(
Deng et al. 2009
)
.
This dataset comprises 14,197,122 images distributed across 21,841 classes.
For training, each RGB image was resized to dimensions
3\times 224\times 224
, normalized using means of
(0.485,0.456,0.406)
and standard deviations of
(0.229,0.224,0.225)
, and then subjected to center cropping and random flipping.
We set the mini-batch size as 4,096 and apply the Lion optimizer
(
Chen et al. 2023c
)
with learning rate
9.6\times 10^{-5}
and weight decay
0.05
.
All the models, including
crate
s and ViTs are pre-trained with 90 epochs on ImageNet-21K.
For fine-tuning on downstream tasks, we use the AdamW optimizer
Loshchilov and Hutter 2019
and configure the learning rate to
5\times 10^{-5}
, weight decay as 0.01. Due to memory constraints, we set the batch size to be 128 for all experiments conducted for the base models and set it to be 256 for the other smaller models.

As summarized in Table
14
, we evaluate transfer learning performance of
crate
by fine-tuning models that are pre-trained on ImageNet-21k for the following downstream vision classification tasks: ImageNet-1k
(
Deng et al. 2009
)
, CIFAR10/CIFAR100
(
Krizhevsky et al. 2009
)
, Oxford Flowers-102
(
Nilsback and Zisserman 2008
)
, Oxford-IIIT-Pets
(
Parkhi et al. 2012
)
. We also evaluate the transfer learning fine-tuning performance of two ViT models (-T/8 and -B/8) pre-trained on ImageNet-21K for reference. For both
crate
and ViT models, we consider the image patch with size
8\times 8
.

C.6
Ablation Study of ISTA Layer in CRATE

We study the role of the ISTA block in our white-box architecture design. Specifically, we remove the ISTA block in
crate
and compare its performance with the default
crate
in both vision and language tasks.
The results are summarized in Table
15
and
16
.
We can find that the constructed white-box transformers without the ISTA block is able to achieve non-trivial performance on ImageNet.
On the other hand, the white-box transformer
crate
(with the ISTA sparsity block) achieves much better performance than
crate
without the ISTA block, demonstrating the effectiveness of the sparsity block in our architecture design.

Both the attention block (
MSSA
) and the MLP block (
ISTA
) in
crate
are different from the ones in the ViT.
In order to understand the effect of each component of
crate
, we study three different variants of
crate
:
crate
,
crate
-
MHSA
,
crate
-
MLP
, where we denote the attention block and MLP block in ViT as
MHSA
and
MLP
respectively. We summarize different model architectures in
Table

17
.

For all models in
Table

17
, we first apply the same pre-training setup on the ImageNet-21k dataset. The detection and segmentation results are evaluated on models pre-trained on ImageNet-21k. Secondly, we further fine-tune the ImageNet-21k pre-trained models on ImageNet-1k with 50 epochs and summarize the accuracy of different models in Table
Table

17
.
We then apply the coarse segmentation evaluation and MaskCut evaluation (described in
Section

4.3.2
) to quantitatively compare the performance of different models.
As shown in
Table

17
,
crate
significantly outperforms other model architectures across all tasks.
Interestingly, we find that the coarse segmentation performance (i.e., VOC Seg) of the ViT can be significantly improved by simply replacing the
MHSA
in ViT with the
MSSA
in
crate
, despite the architectural differences between
MHSA
and
MSSA
being small.
This demonstrates the effectiveness of the white-box design.

C.9
Additional Experimental Results of Evaluating Compression and Sparsity for ViT

We conduct experiments to evaluate the compression (
R^{c}
) and sparsity (
\ell^{0}
) of token representations from each layer of a pre-trained ViT-Base (downloaded from
https://github.com/huggingface/pytorch-image-models
).
We summarize the results in Figure
26
.
We find that without our white-box design, the vanilla ViT does not optimize our proposed sparse rate reduction objective.
This contrasts with the results shown in Figures
14
and
15
of the work, wherein we can observe that the compression term
R^{c}
and sparsity value decrease layerwise for
crate
, in accordance with our theory.

Figure 26
:

Left
: The compression term
R^{c}(\bm{Z}^{\ell+1/2})
of the multi-head self-attention outputs at different layers.
Middle
: The sparsity of outputs of the
MLP
block,
\|\bm{Z}^{\ell+1}\|_{0}/(d\cdot N)
, at different layers.
Right
: To get a more fine-grained understanding of the sparsity of
MLP
block outputs of ViT, we use three different thresholds
\tau\in\{1.0,0.5,0.1\}
and measure
\sum_{i,j}\mathbf{1}\{|\bm{Z}^{\ell+1}_{i,j}|<\tau\}/(d\cdot N)
, where
\bm{Z}^{\ell+1}_{i,j}
represents the
j
-th element in the
i
-th token representation.
(First row model: ViT-Small; second row model: ViT-Base; third row model: our proposed
crate
-Small).

C.10
Details and Experimental Results of Attention Map Visualization

We recapitulate the method to visualize attention maps in
Abnar and Zuidema 2020
;
Caron et al. 2021
, at first specializing their use to instances of the
crate
model before generalizing to the ViT.

For the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
head at the
\ell^{\mathrm{th}}
layer of
crate
, we compute the
self-attention matrix

\bm{A}_{k}^{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined as follows:

\bm{A}_{k}^{\ell}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{k,1}^{\ell}\\
\vdots\\
A_{k,N}^{\ell}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},\quad\text{where}\quad A_{k,i}^{\ell}=\frac{\exp(\langle\bm{U}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}^{\ell}_{i},\bm{U}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}^{\ell}_{\texttt{[CLS]}}\rangle)}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\exp(\langle\bm{U}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}^{\ell}_{j},\bm{U}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}^{\ell}_{\texttt{[CLS]}}\rangle)}.

(477)

We then reshape the attention matrix
\bm{A}_{k}^{\ell}
into a
{\sqrt{n}\times\sqrt{n}}
matrix and visualize the heatmap as shown in
Figure

20
.
For example, the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
row and the
j^{\mathrm{th}}
column element of the heatmap in
Figure

20
corresponds to the
m^{\mathrm{th}}
component of
\bm{A}_{k}^{\ell}
if
m=(i-1)\cdot\sqrt{n}+j
.
In
Figure

20
, we select 4 attention heads of
crate
and visualize the attention matrix
\bm{A}_{k}^{\ell}
for each image.

For the ViT, the entire methodology remains the same, except that the attention map is defined in the following reasonable way:

\bm{A}_{k}^{\ell}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{k,1}^{\ell}\\
\vdots\\
A_{k,N}^{\ell}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},\quad\text{where}\quad A_{k,i}^{\ell}=\frac{\exp(\langle\bm{K}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}^{\ell}_{i},\bm{Q}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}^{\ell}_{\texttt{[CLS]}}\rangle)}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\exp(\langle\bm{K}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}^{\ell}_{j},\bm{Q}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}^{\ell}_{\texttt{[CLS]}}\rangle)}.

(478)

where the “query” and “key” parameters of the standard transformer at head
k
and layer
\ell
are denoted
\bm{K}_{k}^{\ell}
and
\bm{Q}_{k}^{\ell}
respectively.

Details about MaskCut.

We apply the MaskCut pipeline (Algorithm
1
) to generate segmentation masks and detection bounding box (discussed in
Section

4.3.2
).
As described by
Wang et al. 2023b
, we iteratively apply Normalized Cuts
(
Shi and Malik 2000
)
on the patch-wise affinity matrix
\bm{M}^{\ell}
, where
\bm{M}_{ij}^{\ell}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\langle\bm{U}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}_{i}^{\ell},\bm{U}^{\ell*}_{k}\bm{z}_{j}^{\ell}\rangle
.
At each iterative step, we mask out the identified patch-wise entries on
\bm{M}^{\ell}
. To obtain more fine-grained segmentation masks, MaskCut employs Conditional Random Fields (CRF)
(
Krähenbühl and Koltun 2011
)
to post-process the masks, which smooths the edges and filters out unreasonable masks. Correspondingly, the detection bounding box is defined by the rectangular region that tightly encloses a segmentation mask.

1

Hyperparameter:

S
, the number of objects to segment.

2

Function

MaskCut(
\bm{M}
)
:

3

for

i\in\{1,\dots,S\}

do

4

mask

\leftarrow

NCut
(
\bm{M}
) ;

//
mask
is a boolean array

\bm{M}\leftarrow\bm{M}\odot\texttt{mask}
;

//
Equivalent to applying the mask to
\bm{M}

masks
[i]
\leftarrow

mask
;

5

endfor

6

7

return

masks

8

Algorithm 1

MaskCut

Additional visualization of attention map.

We provide additional experiments on comparing the attention maps of
crate
and ViT in
Figure

27
.

Figure 27
:
More attention maps of supervised
crate
and ViT
on images from COCO val2017. We select the second-to-last layer attention maps to visualize for
crate
and the last layer for ViT.
